Effective behaviour of critical-contrast PDEs: micro-resonances, frequency conversion, and time dispersive properties. II.
Kirill Cherednichenko, Alexander Kiselev, Igor Velčić, Josip Žubrinić

Abstract
For the system of equations of linear elasticity with periodic coefficients displaying high contrast, in the regime of resonant scaling between the material properties of the “soft” part of the medium and the spatial period, we construct an asymptotic approximation exhibiting time-dispersive properties of the medium and prove associated order-sharp error estimates in the sense of the L 2 → L 2 L^{2}\to L^{2} operator norm. The analytic framework presented can be viewed as an advanced version of the one presented in [Effective behaviour of critical-contrast PDEs: micro-resonances, frequency conversion, and time dispersive properties. I. Commun. Math. Phys. 375, 1833–1884], where the corresponding setup of a scalar PDEs was addressed. Keywords Homogenisation ⋅ \cdot Resonant composites ⋅ \cdot Resolvent asymptotics ⋅ \cdot Metamaterials ⋅ \cdot Wave propagation Mathematics Subject Classification (2020): Primary 35Q99; Secondary 47F05, 47N50, 35B27, 47A10, 81U30



1
Introduction

“Quantitative” asymptotic methods in the analysis of parameter-dependent families of PDEs (in particular, multiscale asymptotic analysis), see e.g.
[
41
,
3
,
21
,
43
,
23
]
, have emerged recently as a natural improvement of the classical semi-formal ad-hoc asymptotic approach. Their key feature is pursuit of an
estimate
on the difference between the “exact” (usually inaccessible) solution to the wave propagation problem and its asymptotic approximation in terms of some non-dimensional parameters, e.g. the ratio
\varepsilon
between the wavelength and the period of material oscillations. This idea has brought forth the physically relevant possibility to account for degenerate problems (such as the “flipped double-porosity” setup
[
10
]
), which are both technically more challenging and generally not amenable to the said classical approach. On the physical side of the issue, the principal application of the related analysis is to the development of a rigorous understanding of metamaterials
[
7
]
. It can be argued, see e.g. the discussion in
[
12
]
, that generically metamaterial behaviour is due to the “corrector” terms in the relevant asymptotic expansion
[
10
]
. Indeed, if one assumed that the family admits a “limiting” operator in a strong enough topology, then the latter must inherit the defining properties of the family, which would not permit the non-trivial effects expected of a metamaterial. This calls for a new outlook on the asymptotic analysis of operator families that would lead to quantitatively tight asymptotic expansions
capturing fully those features of the solution that are of value for specific physical problems at hand.

In connection with this goal, operator theory has emerged as a source of powerful tools, due to its general functional-analytic framework for bounding the norm of the solution in terms of the norm of the problem data, in
other words estimating the
resolvent
.
Indeed, a typical consequence of the related bound is the guarantee that the resolvent of the operator associated with the approximation is close, in a quantitatively controlled fashion, to the resolvent of the original, “inaccessible”, operator. The availability of these operator-theoretic tools is complemented by the interest in using resonant elements within composite media for controlling wave motion and by recent advances in the mathematical analysis of boundary-value problems for differential equations.
Indeed, for problems involving natural length-scale ratios such as the parameter
\varepsilon
mentioned above — another example being heterogeneous “thin” structures (rods, plates, shells, and their combinations), where a similar non-dimensional parameter may represent the ratio of the structure thickness to the typical length of variation of material properties
[
15
,
6
,
16
]
— the relevant geometric information can be translated into a parametrised family of “transmission” or “boundary value” problems for partial differential equations (PDE), for which an analysis of the “asymptotically leading” part of the solution behaviour can be carried out. For linear setups, the analytical access to this leading-order term is afforded by a spectral decomposition, either for the problem itself or for its appropriate reformulation. In particular, in
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
we used the link (known in operator theory as the “Krein formula”) between the resolvent and the Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) operator, or the “
M
-matrix”, to obtain sharp operator-norm convergence estimates in the “high-contrast” setup for a scalar elliptic PDE describing a composite containing “resonating” components, which admit rapidly oscillating waves — the related setup has often been referred to in the literature as “double-porosity model”
[
2
,
1
,
42
]
. An “asymptotic diagonalisation” of the DtN map allowed us to quantify the associated resonant effect and formulate an approximating model of a “non-classical” type. Prior to the work
[
8
]
, all techniques for the analysis of resonant media relied on weaker notions of convergence (e.g. the so-called two-scale convergence
[
1
]
), which do not allow one to achieve the same degree of control of the solution, i.e. an order-explicit estimate of its
L^{2}
norm by the similar norm of the problem data. The paper
[
8
]
introduced the idea of developing power-series asymptotic approximations (in terms of powers of
\varepsilon
) that are
uniform
with respect to the wave-length
2\pi/\chi
in a spatial integral representation of
L^{2}
functions (such as the Bloch-Floquet-Gelfand representation, for which
\chi
is often referred to as “quasimomentum”). These were then used to obtain operator-norm estimates for scalar problems with high-contrast, thereby providing a quantitative version of the result of
[
42
]
. The same idea of using power-series expansion for the decomposition by wave-length was applied in
[
15
,
16
]
in the context of simultaneous homogenisation and dimension reduction for elastic plates and rods, where power series in terms of the quasimomentum
\chi
were constructed first and then converted in the order-sharp estimates in terms of the original parameter
\varepsilon.

In the present paper we obtain order-sharp norm-resolvent estimates for
systems
of PDEs with critical contrast, by adopting expansions with respect to the quasimomentum
\chi
cast in the operator-theoretic framework of
[
10
]
. In order to reach a key milestone on the way to the asymptotic analysis of resonant media, namely the so-called Krein formula for the resolvent of the operator in question, we adopt the approach of
[
34
]
via what we call “Ryzhov triple”, which provides ingredients for defining the “M-operator”, i.e. the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map on the interface between the resonant components and the “host” medium, as a function of the spectral parameter. As the vector setup can not be addressed as a simple generalisation of the scalar case, we develop a new method for constructing the Ryzhov triple, based on the existing theory of boundary-value problems for elliptic PDE. In effect, this sheds a new light on the Ryzhov triple and the corresponding Krein formula as natural tools for quantitative studies of boundary-value problems, Notably, the utility of these tools extends well beyond our analysis of critical contrast as inspired by the homogenisation perspective of
[
1
]
and
[
42
]
. In particular, the new framework allows one to re-formulate a number of existing problems in material characterisation and wave propagation in a way that will provide a general methodology for asymptotic approximations with order-sharp error control, for example the problems of scattering of waves on inhomogeneous obstacle, see
[
12
]
and references therein. The second key aspect of the present work is a demonstration that the mentioned Krein formula is amenable to asymptotic analysis with respect to the quasimomentum
\chi
yielding uniform order-sharp error estimates. Overall, the two mentioned analytic features constitute the present, second part of our work on quantitative descriptions of time dispersion in media with resonant inclusions. As a by-product of our analysis, we recover the homogenization result on perforated domain, see Remark
7.6
.
For an additional comparison of the present work with
[
10
]
, see Remark
6.12
. Although here we analyse the elasticity system, it can be seen that the same analysis can be applied to any elliptic system of the form
[
3
]
. It is also worth mentioning that the techniques used here can tackle the situation of stiff inclusion in the soft matrix, as was done in
[
10
]
in the scalar setting.

Next we outline the structure of the paper. In Section
2
, we describe the setup and state the main results (see Theorem
2.2
and Theorem
2.3
).
We also show how to decompose the operator using Gelfand transform and reformulate the resolvent problem as a family of transmission problems. In Section
3
we introduce the abstract framework of boundary triples and we show how to put a transmission problem into this framework.
In Section
4
we construct the asymptotics that we use for the proof of the main results. Section
5
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.2
, Section
6
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.3
(a) and Section
7
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.3
(b) as well as additional result on dispersion relation. Finally, in the Appendix we prove some auxiliary claims needed in the paper.

2
Setup and main results

2.1
Notation

In this section we introduce the notation which we will use throughout the paper.

For a vector
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},
we denote by
a_{j},

j=1,\dots,k
its components.
Similarly, the entries of a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},
are referred to as
A_{ij},

i,j=1,\dots,k,
and
\sym\boldsymbol{A}
denotes the symmetric part of a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
.
The vectors of the standard orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
are denoted by
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,k
. Furthermore, for
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},
we denote by
\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, the matrix with entries
a_{i}b_{j},
and
set
\boldsymbol{a}\odot\boldsymbol{b}:=\sym(\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}).

The inner product of matrices
A,

B
is denoted by
\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{B}:={\rm Tr}(A^{\top}B),
and
\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}_{\rm skew}
(
\mathbb{C}^{k\times k}_{\rm skew}
) stands for the space of antisymmetric matrices with real (complex) entries.

For an operator
{\mathcal{A}}
(or a bilinear form
a
) the domain of
{\mathcal{A}}
(respectively
a
) is denoted by
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}})
(respectively
{\mathcal{D}}(a)
). We use the notation
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
for the closure of a closable operator
\mathcal{A},
and denote by
\sigma({\mathcal{A}})
the spectrum of an operator
\mathcal{A}.
For normed vector spaces
X,Y
, we denote by
\mathfrak{B}(X,Y)
the space of bounded linear operators from
X
to
Y
. Furthermore, when indicating a function space
X
in the notation for a norm
\|\cdot\|_{X},
we omit the physical domain on which functions in
X
are defined whenever it is clear from the context. For example, we often write
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}},

\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}}
instead of
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{k})},

\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{k})},

k\in\mathbb{N}.

For
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
by
{\rm dist}(A,B)
we denote the distance between the sets
A
and
B
. For
f\in L^{1}(A)
,
\overline{f}
denotes
\overline{f}:=\int_{A}f
and
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
denotes the characteristic function of
A
. Finally,
\delta_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker delta.

2.2
The operator of linear elasticity

Consider the “reference cell”
Y:=[0,1)^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3},
and let
Y_{\rm soft}\subset Y
be a connected open set with smooth boundary
\Gamma
(possessing, e.g.,
\mathcal{C}^{1,1}
regularity) such that the closure of
Y_{\rm soft}
is a subset of the interior of
Y
and
Y_{\rm stiff}=Y\setminus Y_{\rm soft}.

For a fixed period of material oscillations
\varepsilon>0
, we would like to study the behaviour of a composite elastic medium with components whose properties are in high contrast to one another. We refer to the component materials as “soft” and “stiff” accordingly. We this goal in mind, we view
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as being composed of two complementary subsets, the stiff part
\Omega_{\rm stiff}^{\varepsilon}
(“matrix”) and the soft complement
\Omega_{\rm soft}^{\varepsilon}
(“inclusions”):

\Omega_{\rm stiff}^{\varepsilon}:=\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\Omega_{\rm soft}^{\varepsilon},\qquad\Omega_{\rm soft}^{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}\bigl\{\varepsilon(Y_{\rm soft}+z)\bigr\}.

We are interested in the approximation properties, when
\varepsilon\to 0
, of the operator family
(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
, where, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the operators
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
are defined as the unique self-adjoint unbounded operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
corresponding to the differential expressions

-\div\bigl(\mathbb{A}^{\varepsilon}(x/\varepsilon)\sym\nabla\bigr)

on domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3}).
These operators are defined by the bilinear forms

a_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbb{A}^{\varepsilon}(x/\varepsilon)\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\quad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}).

The tensors of material coefficients

\mathbb{A}^{\varepsilon}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}(y),&y\in Y_{\rm stiff},\\[2.5pt]
\varepsilon^{2}\,\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}(y),&y\in Y_{\rm soft}\end{array}\right.

are defined on the unit cell
Y
and extended to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
via periodicity. We make the following assumptions about the tensor-valued functions
\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}.

Assumption 2.1
.

•

Uniform positive definiteness and uniform boundedness on symmetric matrices: there exists
\nu>0
such that

\nu|\xi|^{2}\leq\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}(y)\boldsymbol{\xi}:\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq\nu^{-1}|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{\xi}\quad\forall y\in Y.

•

Material symmetries

[\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}]_{ijkl}=[\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}]_{jikl}=[\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}]_{klij},\quad i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3\}.

•

Lipschitz continuity:

[\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}]_{ijkl}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,1}(\overline{Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}}),\quad i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3\}.

We are interested in the approximation properties of the resolvent operator associated with
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
, for small
\varepsilon>0
. Namely, we would like to obtain asymptotics of the resolvents
\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1},
as
\varepsilon\to 0,
in the
L^{2}\to L^{2}
operator norm. The spectral parameter
z\in\mathbb{C}
is assumed uniformly separated from the spectrum of the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}:
more precisely, we fix
\sigma>0
and consider a compact set
K_{\sigma}\subset\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\ {\rm dist}(z,\mathbb{R})\geq\sigma\right\}.

We introduce the following space of functions supported only on the stiff (respectively, soft) component:

L^{\rm stiff(soft)}_{\varepsilon}:=\bigl\{\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}):\ \boldsymbol{f}\equiv 0\mbox{ on }\Omega_{\rm soft(stiff)}^{\varepsilon}\bigr\}.

(1)

Furthermore, we denote by
P^{\rm stiff}_{\varepsilon}
the orthogonal projector from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
onto
L^{\rm stiff}_{\varepsilon}
. In order to state the main theorem, we first define the following operators, which make up crucial components of the leading-order term of the resolvent asymptotics.

Definition 2.1

(Macroscopic operator)
.

Consider the tensor
\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3\times 3\times 3}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\,\xi_{1}:\xi_{2}:=\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}\left(\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}}+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle:\left(\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}}+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\right)

(2)

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\ \boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i},\ i=1,2.

where
\boldsymbol{u}_{\xi}\in H^{1}_{\#}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{R}^{3})
is the unique solution to the problem

\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}\left(\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\xi}+\boldsymbol{\xi}\right):\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}=0\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\#}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{R}^{3}).

We define the macroscopic operator (the operator of perforated domain)
\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}
as the self-adjoint unbounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
corresponding to the differential expression

-\div\left(\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\sym\nabla\cdot\right),

(3)

with domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro})\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
, defined by the bilinear form

a_{\rm macro}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\quad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}).

We will require the following lemma, the proof of which is standard.

Lemma 2.1
.

There exists a constant
\eta>0
such that

\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\boldsymbol{\xi}:\boldsymbol{\xi}\geq\eta|\boldsymbol{\xi}|^{2}
for all
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{\xi},

and
[\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}]_{ijkl}=[\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}]_{jikl}=[\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}]_{klij},

i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3\}.

Proof.

The proof is based on a standard extension argument, see e.g.
[
6
, Proposition 3.4]
. ∎

In addition to the properties highlighted in Lemma
2.1
, the leading-order term in the resolvent asymptotics retains information on the microstructure, via the spectrum of the “Bloch operator”
\mathcal{A}_{\rm Bloch}
associated with the bilinear form

a_{\rm Bloch}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=\int_{Y_{\rm soft}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\quad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(Y_{\rm soft};\mathbb{R}^{3}),

as a nonnegative self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(Y_{\rm soft};\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Furthermore, we define the matrix-valued “Zhikov function”
\mathcal{B}
by

\begin{split}\left[\mathcal{B}(z)\right]_{ij}:=z\delta_{ij}+z^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{i}[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{j}}{\eta_{k}-z},\quad i,j\in{1,2,3},\end{split}

(4)

where
(\eta_{k},\varphi_{k})
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
are the eigenpairs of the associated Bloch operator
\mathcal{A}_{\rm Bloch}
on
L^{2}(Y_{\rm soft};\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
z
is an arbitrary complex number different from all
\eta_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
(an increasing sequence converging to infinity). The above defined operators can be naturally extended to complex-valued functions.
The appearance of the matrix-valued Zhikov function in the approximation of the operator in high-contrast is standard, even in qualitative analysis (see, e.g.
[
44
]
).

2.3
Main results

We will now state the main results of the paper which deal with the approximation of the resolvent of the operator. We will state
\varepsilon^{2}
and
\varepsilon
approximation result. The benefits of
\varepsilon
approximation is that it provides simpler operator that, when restricted to the stiff component, is second order differential operator. On the other hand, the approximation with
\varepsilon^{2}
precision provides the operator which is (even when restricted to the stiff component) pseudodifferential. The operators
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}

and the coresponding projections
\Theta^{\rm app}_{\varepsilon}
,
\Theta^{\rm eff}_{\varepsilon}
are defined in Section
5.3
and Section
6
respectively. The proof of Theorem
2.2
is also given in Section
5.3
and the proof of claim (a) in Theorem
2.3
is given in Section
6
, while claim (b) is proved in Section
7
.

Theorem 2.2
.

For all
z\in K_{\sigma}
one has

\bigl\lVert\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}-(\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app})^{*}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}-zI\bigr)^{-1}\Theta^{\rm app}_{\varepsilon}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

where the constant
C>0
depends only on
\sigma
and
{\rm diam}(K_{\sigma})
. The operator
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
is a self-adjoint operator defined on the subspace
\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
in what follows, and
\Theta^{\rm app}_{\varepsilon}
is a projection.

Theorem 2.3
.

For all
z\in K_{\sigma}
one has

(a)

\bigl\lVert\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}-(\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff})^{*}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}-zI\bigr)^{-1}\Theta^{\rm eff}_{\varepsilon}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon,

where the constant
C>0
depends only on
\sigma
and
{\rm diam}(K_{\sigma})
. The operator
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}
is a self-adjoint operator defined (on the subspace
\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
) in the continuation of the text and
\Theta^{\rm eff}_{\varepsilon}
is a projection.

(b)

\bigl\lVert P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}-P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon,

where
\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}
is a differential operator of linear elasticity with constant coefficients defined by (
3
).

For an additional comment about approximating operators see Remark
7.7
and Remark
6.13
below.
It is well known that the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
has band-gap structure (see
[
42
]
). Theorem
2.2
and Theorem
2.3
enable us to estimate the gaps in the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
on any compact interval with the gaps in the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
, i.e.
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}
.

Corollary 2.4
.

For every
M>0,
one has

\displaystyle{\rm dist}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}})\cap[-M,M],\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app})\cap[-M,M]\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(M+1)^{2}\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle{\rm dist}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}})\cap[-M,M],\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff})\cap[-M,M]\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(M+1)^{2}\varepsilon.

where
C>0
is a fixed constant independent of
M

Proof.

The proof can be obtained by taking
z=i
in Theorem
2.2
and Theorem
2.3
(a).
It is well known that for the self-adjoint bounded linear operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{X}
we have

{\rm dist}\left(\sigma(\mathcal{A}),\sigma(\mathcal{B})\right)\leq\|\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\|_{\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}},

see e.g.
[
22
]
.
We have

\displaystyle\sigma\left(\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-{\rm i}I\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda-{\rm i}}:\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})\right\},

\displaystyle\sigma\left((\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app})^{*}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}-{\rm i}I\right)^{-1})\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda-{\rm i}}:\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm app})\right\},

\displaystyle\sigma\left((\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff})^{*}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}-{\rm i}I\right)^{-1})\Theta_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda-{\rm i}}:\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm eff})\right\}.

The claim follows from the fact that for arbitrary
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
we have:

|\lambda-\mu|=|\lambda-{\rm i}||\mu-{\rm i}|\left|\frac{1}{\lambda-{\rm i}}-\frac{1}{\lambda-i}\right|\leq(|\lambda|+1)(|\mu|+1)\left|\frac{1}{\lambda-{\rm i}}-\frac{1}{\lambda-{\rm i}}\right|.\qed

We also can extend the claim of Theorem
2.2
and
2.3
outside
K_{\sigma}
, provided that we stay away from the spectrum in a bounded region.
We can also aposteriori estimate the dependence of constant
C
on the distance of
z
from the spectrum of the operator
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}
and the modulus of
z
.

Corollary 2.5
.

The claims of Theorem
2.2
can be extended to all
z\notin\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}})\cup\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\rm app}_{{\varepsilon}})
and the constant
C=C(z)
depends on
z
in the following way

C(z)=C\left(1+(|z|+1)/{\rm dist}(z,\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}))\right)\left(1+(|z|+1)/{\rm dist}(z,\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\rm app}_{{\varepsilon}}))\right),

where
C
is independent of
z
.
The analogous claim is valid for Theorem
2.3
(a).

Proof.

For the unbounded operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
on the Banach space
\mathcal{X}
, the orthogonal projection
\mathcal{P}
that commutes with the operator
\mathcal{B}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\notin\sigma(\mathcal{A})\cup\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sigma(\mathcal{B})
it is easy to check the identity

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}-z_{2}I)^{-1}

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{B}-z_{2}I)^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}

(5)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{B}-z_{2}I)^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{B}-z_{1}I){\mathcal{P}}\left((\mathcal{A}-z_{1}I)^{-1}-{\mathcal{P}}(\mathcal{B}-z_{1}I)^{-1}{\mathcal{P}}\right)(\mathcal{A}-z_{1}I)(\mathcal{A}-z_{2}I)^{-1}

\displaystyle+(I-{\mathcal{P}})(\mathcal{A}-z_{1}I)^{-1}(\mathcal{A}-z_{1}I)(\mathcal{A}-z_{2}I)^{-1}.

Notice also that by the functional calculus for a self-adjoint
\mathcal{A}
we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigl\|(\mathcal{A}-z_{1}I)(\mathcal{A}-z_{2}I)^{-1}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}}}\leq 1+\frac{|z_{2}-z_{1}|}{{\rm dist}(z_{2},\sigma\bigl(\mathcal{A})\bigr)}.

We take again
z_{1}={\rm i}
in Theorem
5.10
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}^{\rm app}
and
{\mathcal{P}}=\Theta^{\rm app}
.
Notice that as a consequence of Theorem
2.2
we have

\bigl\lVert(I-\Theta^{\rm app}_{\varepsilon})\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-{\rm i}I\right)^{-1}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

(6)

The claim follows from (
5
)-(
6
).
∎

Remark 2.6
.

By combining Corollary
2.4
and
2.5
one can make the constant
C=C(z)
in Theorem
2.2
and
2.3
dependent only on
|z|
and
{\rm dist}(z,\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}))
, i.e. only on
|z|
and
{\rm dist}(z,\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\rm app/eff}_{{\varepsilon}}))
. By the analysis of the proof of Theorem
2.3
(b) presented in Section
7
the same can be concluded for Theorem
2.3
(b). Also, the fixed constant
C>0
in Corollary
2.4
and
2.5
that corresponds to
z={\rm i}
in Theorem
2.2
and
2.3
can be easily estimated from the proofs of these theorems.
∎

2.4
Gelfand transform and periodic decomposition

The purpose of this chapter is to decompose the original differential operator into a family of differential operators with compact resolvents
that act on functions defined on the unit cell
Y
. This is carried out in a standard way by the Gelfand transform.

Denote by
L_{\#}^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and
H_{\#}^{1}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})
the spaces of
Y
-periodic functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
,
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}),
respectively. The Gelfand transform
\mathcal{G}
is defined on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
by the formula

(\mathcal{G}\boldsymbol{u})(y,\chi):=\left(2\pi\right)^{-3/2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\chi(y+n)}\boldsymbol{u}(y+n),\quad y\in Y,\quad\chi\in Y^{\prime},

where
Y^{\prime}:=[-\pi,\pi)^{3}
. We denote with
x
the variable in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, while we use the notation
y
for the variable in
Y
.
The Gelfand transform is a unitary operator:

\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(Y^{\prime};L_{\#}^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}))=\int_{Y^{\prime}}^{\oplus}L^{2}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3},\chi)d\chi,

in the sense that

\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}=\left\langle\mathcal{G}\boldsymbol{u},\mathcal{G}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(Y^{\prime};L_{\#}^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}))}
for all
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

A function can be reconstructed from its Gelfand transform as follows:

\boldsymbol{u}(x)=(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int_{Y^{\prime}}{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\chi\cdot x}(\mathcal{G}\boldsymbol{u})(x,\chi)d\chi.

For a systematic overview of the properties of Gelfand transform, the reader is encouraged to consult
[
4
]
.

In order to deal with the setting of highly oscillating material coefficients, we consider the following scaled version of Gelfand transform. For a fixed
\varepsilon>0
and all
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
, we set

(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u})(y,\chi):=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi}\right)^{3/2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}}{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\chi(y+n)}\boldsymbol{u}(\varepsilon(y+n)),\quad y\in Y,\ \ \chi\in Y^{\prime}.

The scaled Gelfand transform
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}
transforms functions into
Y
-periodic functions, namely:

(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u})(y+1,\chi)=(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u})(y,\chi)\qquad\quad\forall y\in Y,\ \ \chi\in Y^{\prime}.

Note that the scaled Gelfand transform
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}
is a composition of classical Gelfand transform
\mathcal{G}
and the unitary scaling operator
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
defined by

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}(x):=\varepsilon^{3/2}\boldsymbol{u}(\varepsilon x),\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

It follows that
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}

is also unitary, i.e.

\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}=\left\langle\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u},\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(Y^{\prime};L_{\#}^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}))}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

The original function is recovered from its Gelfand transform by the formula

\boldsymbol{u}(x)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-3/2}\int_{Y^{\prime}}{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\chi\cdot x/\varepsilon}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u})(x/\varepsilon,\chi)d\chi.

(7)

Also, by noting that for the scaled Gelfand transform of a derivative one has

\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{u})=\varepsilon^{-1}\bigl(\partial_{y_{\alpha}}\left(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}\right)+{\rm i}\chi_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}\right)\bigr),\quad\alpha=1,2,3,

we infer that

\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\left(\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\right)(y,\chi)=\varepsilon^{-1}\bigl(\sym\nabla_{y}\left(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}\right)+{\rm i}\sym\left(\left(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}\right)\otimes\chi\right)\bigr)=\varepsilon^{-1}\bigl(\sym\nabla_{y}\left(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}\right)+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\left(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}\right)\bigr),

(8)

where the for each
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
the operator
X_{\chi}
acting on
L^{2}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})
is defined by

X_{\chi}\boldsymbol{u}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{u}\odot\chi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\begin{bmatrix}\chi_{1}u_{1}&\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{1}u_{2}+\chi_{2}u_{1})&\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{1}u_{3}+\chi_{3}u_{1})\\[6.99997pt]
\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{1}u_{2}+\chi_{2}u_{1})&\chi_{2}u_{2}&\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{3}u_{2}+\chi_{2}u_{3})\\[6.99997pt]
\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{3}u_{1}+\chi_{1}u_{3})&\frac{1}{2}(\chi_{3}u_{2}+\chi_{2}u_{3})&\chi_{3}u_{3}\end{bmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

One should note that

C_{1}|\chi|||\boldsymbol{u}||_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq||X_{\chi}\boldsymbol{u}||_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}\leq C_{2}|\chi|||\boldsymbol{u}||_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

With the Gelfand transformation at hand, one can show that

a_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\int_{Y^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{-2}a_{\chi,\varepsilon}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u},\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{v})d\chi,

where

a_{\chi,\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=\int_{Y}\mathbb{A}^{\varepsilon}(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{v}},\qquad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

For each
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon>0,
we introduce the self-adjoint operator

\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}:=(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})^{*}\mathbb{A}^{\varepsilon}(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}):\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})\subset H^{1}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})

associated with the form
a_{\chi,\varepsilon}.
Here we use the notation
(\cdot)^{*}
for the formal adjoint of the operator. Applying the scaled Gelfand transform to the resolvent yields

\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}=\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left(\int_{Y^{\prime}}^{\oplus}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}d\chi\right)\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon},\quad z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}),

(9)

which is an example of the classical von Neumann ”fibre decomposition” formula. Due to the compactness of the embedding
H^{1}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})
, the resolvents
\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}
are, in fact, compact. We interpret (
9
) as follows: by applying the Gelfand transform to the problem, we have decomposed the resolvent operator
\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}
into a continuum family of resolvent operators
\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}
indexed by
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
. In contrast to the original resolvent operator, this family consists of compact operators, which have discrete spectra.

2.4.1
The fibers of the resolvent problem

For each
\varepsilon>0,
the resolvent problem
\chi
-fibre (
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
) of the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
consists in finding, for a fixed

z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})
and every
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}_{\#}(Y,\mathbb{C}^{3}),
the solution
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})
to the equation

\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}-zI\right)\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f}.

This is equivalent to the following problem.

Definition 2.2

(Transmission boundary value problem)
.

Find
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})
such that
\boldsymbol{u}|_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\in H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff},\mathbb{C}^{3})
,
\boldsymbol{u}|_{Y_{\rm soft}}\in H^{1}(Y_{\rm soft},\mathbb{C}^{3}),
and

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle\mbox{on $Y_{\rm stiff}$, }

(10)

\displaystyle\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f}

\displaystyle\mbox{on $Y_{\rm soft}$, }

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{+}=\boldsymbol{u}_{-}

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle on}\ \Gamma,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}_{+}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{n}}_{+}+\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}_{-}\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{n}}_{-}=0

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle on}\ \Gamma.

The subscripts “
+
” and “
-
” refer to the (spatial) limits of from inside
Y_{\rm stiff}
and
Y_{\rm soft},
respectively, while
\vec{\boldsymbol{n}}_{+}
,
\vec{\boldsymbol{n}}_{-}
denote the outward pointing unit normals to
\Gamma
from
Y_{\rm stiff}
and
Y_{\rm soft},
respectively.

3
Operator theoretic approach - Ryzhov triples

The purpose of this section is to introduce the abstract framework for the problem (
10
). In Section
3.1
we develop the abstract framework, while in Section
3.2
and Section
3.3
we show how transmission problem can be seen as part of this abstract framework, proving also the main properties of the operators appearing.
First we prepare the necessities needed in order to work under the paradigm of Ryzhov triples, a suited version of boundary triples for the analysis of boundary value problems.

3.1
Abstract notion of Ryzhov triples

The concept of Ryzhov triple was introduced in
[
33
,
34
]
. The main results of this section are Theorem
3.8
which expresses the solution of abstract Robin boundary value problem and Theorem
3.9
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathcal{E}
an auxiliary Hilbert space. Let:

•

\mathcal{A}_{0}
be a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
with
0\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{0})
,

•

\Pi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{H}
be a bounded operator such that

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\cap\mathcal{R}(\Pi)=\{0\},

\ker(\Pi)=\{0\}.

•

\Lambda
be a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{E}
with domain
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)\subset\mathcal{E}
.

We then refer to the triple
\left(\mathcal{A}_{0},\Pi,\Lambda\right)
as a Ryzhov triple on
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E})
.

Our intention is to study boundary value problems, and we introduce the following objects.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space,
\mathcal{E}
an auxiliary Hilbert space, and
\left(\mathcal{A}_{0},\Pi,\Lambda\right)
a Ryzhov triple on
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E})
. Define the operators
{\mathcal{A}},

\Gamma_{0},

\Gamma_{1}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\dot{+}\Pi(\mathcal{E}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi\boldsymbol{g}\to\boldsymbol{f},\quad\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H},\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E},

(11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\Gamma_{0}):=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\dot{+}\Pi(\mathcal{E}),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}:\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi\boldsymbol{g}\to\boldsymbol{g},\quad\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H},\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\Gamma_{1}):=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\dot{+}\Pi(\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}:\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi\boldsymbol{g}\to\Pi^{*}\boldsymbol{f}+\Lambda\boldsymbol{g},\quad\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H},\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{D}(\Lambda).

We say that
(\mathcal{A},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is the boundary triple associated with the Ryzhov triple
\left(\mathcal{A}_{0},\Pi,\Lambda\right)
.

Notice that, by definition, we have

\Lambda\boldsymbol{g}=\Gamma_{1}\Pi\boldsymbol{g}\quad\forall\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{D}(\Lambda),\qquad\Pi^{*}\boldsymbol{f}=\Gamma_{1}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}\quad\forall\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H}.

(12)

In the above definition, the operators
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{1}
typically play the rôles of the trace of a function and of its co-normal derivative on the boundary, respectively. The following result is then well expected and can be found in
[
34
]
.

Theorem 3.1

(Green’s formula)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{0},\Pi,\Lambda)
be a Ryzhov triple, and consider the corresponding boundary triple
(\mathcal{A}
,
\Gamma_{0}
,
\Gamma_{1}).
Then one has

\left\langle\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\left\langle\Gamma_{1}\boldsymbol{u},\Gamma_{0}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}-\left\langle\Gamma_{0}\boldsymbol{u},\Gamma_{1}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\dot{+}\Pi\bigl(\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)\bigr).

(13)

Definition 3.3
.

Suppose
z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{0})
. Define the operator
S(z)
mapping
\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E}
to the solution
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{D}(\Gamma_{0})
of the spectral boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u}=z\boldsymbol{u},\\[2.5pt]
\Gamma_{0}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{g}.\end{array}\right.

The operator-valued function
M(z)
defined on
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
by

M(z)\boldsymbol{:}=\Gamma_{1}S(z),

is called the Weyl
M
-function of the Ryzhov triple
(\mathcal{A}_{0},\Pi,\Lambda)
.

In
[
34
]
the following formulae for the operators
S(z)
and
M(z)
were proven (
z\in\rho({\mathcal{A}}_{0})
):

S(z)=\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\Pi,\qquad M(z)=\Gamma_{1}\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\Pi.

(14)

Note also that

\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}=I+z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}=I+z(\mathcal{A}_{0}-zI)^{-1},

(15)

and therefore

S(z)=\Pi+z(\mathcal{A}_{0}-zI)^{-1}\Pi.

(16)

The next proposition collects some properties of the
M
-function.

Proposition 3.2
.

(Properties of the Weyl
M
-function)

•

The following representation holds:

M(z)=\Lambda+z\Pi^{*}\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\Pi,\quad z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{0}).

(17)

•

M(z)
is an analytic operator-valued function with values in the set of closed operators on
\mathcal{E}
with (
z
-independent) domain
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
.

•

For
z,\xi\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{0})
the operator
M(z)-M(\xi)
is bounded, and one has

M(z)-M(\xi)=(z-\xi)\left(S(\overline{z})\right)^{*}S(\xi).

•

For
\boldsymbol{u}\in\ker(\mathcal{A}-zI)\cap\left\{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\dot{+}\Pi\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)\right\}
, the following formula holds:

M(z)\Gamma_{0}\boldsymbol{u}=\Gamma_{1}\boldsymbol{u}.

(18)

Remark 3.3
.

In the case when the operators
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{1}
the operators of the trace of a function and of the trace of its co-normal derivative, the formula (
18
) clearly reveals the
M
-function
M(z)
to be the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map associated with the resolvent problem.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Due to the fact that
\mathcal{D}(M(z))=\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
independently on
z\in\mathbb{C}
, one can define
the operators

\Re M(z)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(M(z)+M(z)^{*}\bigr),\quad\Im M(z)=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}}\bigl(M(z)-M(z)^{*})

on
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
. Note that by (
17
) and the fact that
\Lambda
is self-adjoint, one has
M(z)^{*}=M(\overline{z}),
and therefore

\Im M(z)=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}}\bigl(M(z)-M(z)^{*}\bigr)=\Im z\left(S(\overline{z})\right)^{*}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S(\overline{z})\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\quad\Im M(z)=\Im(M(z)-\Lambda)=\Im(M(z)-M(0)).

(19)

∎

Remark 3.5
.

It is clear from (
17
) and (
15
) that

M(z)=\Lambda+z\left(\Pi^{*}\Pi\right)+z^{2}\left(\Pi^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{0}-zI)^{-1}\Pi\right).

(20)

This formula will prove to be one of the key elements in deriving the asymptotics, as
\varepsilon\to 0,
of the resolvents
\left(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}
of the operators
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
introduced in Section
2.4
.
∎

For a given
\mathcal{A}_{0},
we define
\mathcal{A}_{00}
to be the restriction of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
to the set
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{00}):=\ker(\Gamma_{0})\cap\ker(\Gamma_{1})
. We refer to
\mathcal{A}_{00}
as the
minimal operator.

Remark 3.6
.

It was shown in
[
34
]
that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{00})
does not actually depend on the choice of the operator
\Gamma_{1}
(or
\Lambda
) but instead can be characterised as the subspace of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})
consisting of those elements
\boldsymbol{u}
for which
\mathcal{A}_{0}\boldsymbol{u}
is orthogonal to the range of
\Pi
.
∎

One can characterize all densely defined closed extensions of
\mathcal{A}_{00}
contained in
\mathcal{A}
to be the operators
\mathcal{A}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}
associated with the spectral boundary value problem

\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f}

subject to an abstract Robin-type condition

\left(\beta_{0}\Gamma_{0}+\beta_{1}\Gamma_{1}\right)\boldsymbol{u}=0,

(21)

by varying over the choice of the operators
\beta_{0}
,
\beta_{1}
on
\mathcal{E}
. The rigorous definition of the extension operators
\mathcal{A}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}
is postponed to Theorem
3.9
. Note that
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is then the self-adjoint extension of
\mathcal{A}_{00}
corresponding to the choice
\beta_{0}=I
,
\beta_{1}=0
.
However, in order to clarify the meaning of (
21
), it is necessary to make additional assumptions on the operators
\beta_{0},\beta_{1}
, e.g., as follows.

Assumption 3.1
.

The operators
\beta_{0},\beta_{1}
are linear operators in
\mathcal{E}
such that
\beta_{0}
is defined on the domain
\mathcal{D}(\beta_{0})\supset\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
and
\beta_{1}
is defined everywhere on
\mathcal{E}
and bounded. The operator
\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}\Lambda
, defined on
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda)
is closable in
\mathcal{E}
.

Under the above assumption, the operator
\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}M(z)
is closable,
and we denote its closure by
\mathcal{B}
. The condition
(
21
)
is shown to be well posed on a certain Hilbert space associated with the closure of
\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}\Lambda
in
\mathcal{E}
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space,
\mathcal{E}
an auxiliary Hilbert space, and
\left(\mathcal{A}_{0},\Pi,\Lambda\right)
a Ryzhov triple on
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E})
. Suppose also that
\beta_{0},\beta_{1}
are linear operators on
\mathcal{E}
satisfying Assumption
3.1
. Consider the space

\mathcal{H}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}:=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\dot{+}\Pi(\mathcal{D}\bigl(\mathcal{B})\bigr)=\{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi\boldsymbol{g}:\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H},\ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\},

equipped with the norm

\left\lVert\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}}:=\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{f}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}+\left\lVert\mathcal{B}\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

The following lemma is also proved in
[
34
]
.

Lemma 3.7
.

The space
(\mathcal{H}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}})
is a Hilbert space. The operator
\beta_{0}\Gamma_{0}+\beta_{1}\Gamma_{1}:\mathcal{H}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}\to\mathcal{E}
is bounded.

We are now in a position to assign a meaning to
the abstract spectral Robin-type boundary value problem and establish its well-posedness.

Theorem 3.8
.

Suppose that
z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{0})
is such that the operator
\overline{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}M(z)}
is boundedly invertible in
\mathcal{E}
. Then, for given
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H},

\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E},
the unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}
to the Robin boundary-value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f},\\[3.00003pt]
\left(\beta_{0}\Gamma_{0}+\beta_{1}\Gamma_{1}\right)\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{g},\end{array}\right.,

is provided by the formula

\boldsymbol{u}=(\mathcal{A}_{0}-zI)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\Pi(\overline{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}M(z)})^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{g}-\beta_{1}\Pi^{*}\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}\right).

(22)

By plugging
\boldsymbol{g}=0,
it is seen that (
22
) defines the resolvent of a closed, densely defined operator in
\mathcal{H}
that extends the minimal operator
\mathcal{A}_{00}
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Suppose that
z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{0})
be such that the operator
\overline{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}M(z)}
defined on
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})
is boundedly invertible in
\mathcal{E}
. Then the operator
\mathcal{R}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}(z)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}(z):=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{0}-zI)^{-1}-\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\Pi(\overline{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}M(z)})^{-1}\beta_{1}\bigl(\Pi^{*}\left(I-z\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}\right)^{-1}\bigr)

(23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{0}-zI)^{-1}-S(z)(\overline{\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}M(z)})^{-1}\beta_{1}S^{*}(\overline{z}).

is the resolvent

\left(\mathcal{A}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}-zI\right)^{-1}
of a closed densely defined operator
\mathcal{A}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}
in
\mathcal{H}

such that

\mathcal{A}_{00}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}}\subset\mathcal{A},\quad\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\beta_{0},\beta_{1}})\subset\ker(\beta_{0}\Gamma_{0}+\beta_{1}\Gamma_{1}).

Remark 3.10
.

Notice that the pointwise nature of Theorem
3.8
, with respect to the parameter
z
, yields the same formula (
22
) under an even more general assumption that the operator
\beta_{1}
depends on
z
. This remark is crucial for the remainder of the paper.
∎

3.2
Operators associated with boundary value problems

In this section we introduce some operators required to reformulate the transmission boundary value problem (
10
) in the context of the abstract theory of Ryzhov triples. First we define the spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle:=L_{\#}^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}),\quad\mathcal{E}:=L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{\boldsymbol{u}\in L_{\#}^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}\equiv 0\mbox{ on }Y_{\rm soft}\bigr\},\quad\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}:=\bigl\{\boldsymbol{u}\in L_{\#}^{2}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}\equiv 0\mbox{ on }Y_{\rm stiff}\bigr\},

as well as the associated orthogonal projections

P_{\rm soft}:\mathcal{H}\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft},

P_{\rm stiff}:\mathcal{H}\mapsto\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}.

Note that the following orthogonal decomposition holds:

\mathcal{H}=P_{\rm soft}\mathcal{H}\oplus P_{\rm stiff}\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}.

(24)

3.2.1
Differential operators of linear elasticity

Relative to the decomposition (
24
), we define self-adjoint operators
\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}
,
\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}
on the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}
,
\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}
, respectively, by the sesquilinear forms

\displaystyle a_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle:=\int_{Y_{\rm soft}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{v}},\quad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}),

(25)

\displaystyle a_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle:=\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{v}},\quad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft})

\displaystyle:=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in H_{\#}^{1}(Y_{\rm soft};\mathbb{C}^{3}),\,\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Gamma}=0\},\quad\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}):=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in H_{\#}^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3}),\,\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Gamma}=0\}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}

correspond to the differential expressions

\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\quad\mbox{ on $\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)}$},

(26)

with zero boundary condition on
\Gamma
. The following proposition is easy to prove.

Proposition 3.11
.

The forms
a_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
are uniformly coercive, symmetric and closed.

Proof.

Due to Assumption
2.1
, there exist
\chi
-independent constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{1}\left\lVert\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}^{2}\leq a_{0,\chi}^{\rm(stiff)soft}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u})\leq C_{2}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm(stiff)soft}).

Furthermore, due to (
122
) there exists a
\chi
-independent
C>0
such that

C\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})}^{2}\leq a_{0,\chi}^{\rm(stiff)soft}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u})\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm(stiff)soft}),

so the forms in question are uniformly coercive.
∎

3.2.2
Lift operators

The way we introduce the lift operators required for the analysis of boundary value problems via Krein’s formula is the following.
First, we introduce classical lift operators
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
as the operators mapping
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
to the weak solutions
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{\#}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})
of the boundary value problems

\begin{split}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}=0\quad\mbox{ on $Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}$},\\[3.50006pt]
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{g}\quad\mbox{ on $\Gamma,$ }\\[3.50006pt]
\mbox{ $\boldsymbol{u}$ is $Y$-periodic. }\end{array}\right.\end{split}

(27)

As the consequence of Proposition
8.8
, the following statement holds.

Proposition 3.12
.

For every
\chi\in Y
the problem (
27
) has a unique weak solution. The associated solution operator
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
is bounded and satisfies the following bounds for all
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}):

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)},\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\bigr\rVert_{H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})},

(28)

where
C>0
is independent of
\chi
.

Proof.

The proof uses the classical approach of rewriting the problem (
27
) with zero boundary condition and non-zero right-hand side and using Proposition
8.3
and Proposition
8.8
. ∎

We would like to introduce operators
\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
as the extensions of
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
to
{\mathcal{E}}=L^{2}(\Gamma;{\mathbb{C}}^{3})
. We do so by defining their adjoints first. To this end, we consider the operators

-\partial^{\rm stiff(soft)}_{\nu}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\bigr)^{-1}=:\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}:\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\to{\mathcal{E}}.

(29)

Here
\partial^{\rm stiff(soft)}_{\nu}
is the trace of the co-normal derivative:

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial^{\rm stiff(soft)}_{\nu}\boldsymbol{u}:=\bigl(\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\cdot\vec{\boldsymbol{n}}_{\rm stiff(soft)}|_{\Gamma},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

where
\vec{\boldsymbol{n}}_{\rm stiff(soft)}
is outward unit normal on
\Gamma
from
Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}
.
Furthermore, we denote by
\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
the adjoint of
\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
with respect to the pair of inner products
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)}}
and
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}
, namely

\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)},\qquad\bigl\langle\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{f}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)}}=\bigl\langle\boldsymbol{g},\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{f}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}.

Theorem 3.13
.

The operators
\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
defined by (
29
) are compact. Their adjoints
\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
are bounded extensions to
\mathcal{E}
of the classical lift operators
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)},
and

\ker\bigl(\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\bigr)=\left\{0\right\},\qquad\mathcal{D}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\bigr)\cap\mathcal{R}\bigl(\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\bigr)=\{0\}.

(30)

Proof.

Due to results on elliptic regularity, under Assumption
2.1
the operators
(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})^{-1}
are bounded from
\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
to
H^{2}(\Omega_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})
by Lemma
8.14
. Thus, using the trace theorem, we infer that
\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
is bounded as an operator with values in
H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

Due to the compactness of the embedding
H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
, it follows that
\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
is compact. Next, one can easily verify that for
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
one has

\bigl\langle\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{f}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)}}=\Bigl\langle\boldsymbol{g},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial^{\rm stiff(soft)}_{\nu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\bigr)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}\Bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{E}},

and hence

\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\bigr|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}=\widetilde{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}.

Proceeding to the proof of (
30
), note that

\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}=0\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E},

holds in the sense of distributions.
In other words, for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})
one has

\int_{Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}}\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{v}=0,

which can be seen through approximation. Under the condition
\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})
, we then also have

\int_{Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{v}=0\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3}),

from which it follows immediately that
\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}=0
. This proves the second property in (
30
).

To prove the first property in (
30
), choose an arbitrary
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and let
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})
be such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial^{\rm stiff(soft)}_{\nu}\boldsymbol{u}=-\boldsymbol{g}&\quad\mbox{ on $\Gamma$},\\[2.5pt]
\boldsymbol{u}=0&\quad\mbox{ on $\Gamma,$ }\\[2.5pt]
\mbox{$\boldsymbol{u}$ is $Y$-periodic. }\end{array}\right.

The existence of such
\boldsymbol{u}
is guaranteed by the Lemma
8.16
. Now, denoting

\boldsymbol{f}:=\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff(soft)},

it is clear that

\boldsymbol{g}=\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{f},

and, due to the density of
H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
in
\mathcal{E}
, we conclude that

\ker(\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\Xi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})}^{\perp}=\left\{0\right\}.

∎

3.2.3
Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps

Here we define the operators
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
and prove their self-adjointness. The main result is Theorem
3.14
. Due to the elliptic regularity, under Assumption
2.1
one can define the operators

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}:H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),\quad\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}=-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{\nu}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{u},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

where
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and
\boldsymbol{u}
is the unique solution to (
27
). Notice that as a consequence of Lemma
8.14
we have

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}))}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{g}\|_{H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(31)

where the constant
C
is independent of
\chi.
We have the following.

Theorem 3.14
.

The operators
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
can be uniquely extended to unbounded nonpositive self-adjoint operators
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
on
\mathcal{E}
with domains
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})=H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
. Additionally,
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
have compact resolvents.

Proof.

The first step of the proof consists in obtaining a larger extension of the operator
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
for which the desired operator
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
is simply a restriction onto
H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
. The second step is to show that this restriction is, in fact, self-adjoint. For this, it suffices to show that the restriction is symmetric, and that the resolvent set of the restriction contains at least one real number, for example
1
.

We begin by taking
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),

\boldsymbol{h}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and considering the solutions
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3}),

\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})
to the problem (
27
) such that

\boldsymbol{u}=\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g},

\boldsymbol{v}=\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{h}.

Using the Green’s formula, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\boldsymbol{h}

\displaystyle=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{v}

(32)

\displaystyle=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{h}.

It follows that
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}
defines an element of
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
. Due to (
28
), the bound

\bigl\lVert\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\bigr\rVert_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})},

(33)

holds, with
C
independent of
\chi\in Y^{\prime}.
In particular, we can define a unique bounded extension

\overline{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}:H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to H^{-1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),\quad\overline{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}|_{H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}.

Since
\left(H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\right)
is an interpolation pair with respect to the pairs
\left(H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\right)
and
\left(H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),H^{-1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\right)
, we conclude that
\overline{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}

is bounded as an operator from
H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
to
L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and denote by
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
the restriction of
\overline{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
to
H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
.

Proceeding to the second step, we prove that
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
is self-adjoint as an unbounded operator on
L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
with domain

\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})=H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

Notice that the
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
is non-positive and symmetric. Indeed, as a consequence of (
32
) holding for
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),

\boldsymbol{h}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),
one has

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\boldsymbol{h}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\cdot

\displaystyle\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{h}

\displaystyle\forall\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{h}\in H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

In order to prove self-adjointness of
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
, it suffices to show that
\rho(\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})\cap\mathbb{R}\neq\emptyset
. We claim that
(-\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}+I)^{-1}:L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
is bounded. To see this, first assume that
\boldsymbol{h}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and seek the solution
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
to the problem

-\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}+\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{h}.

This is equivalent to seeking
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}=0&\mbox{ on $Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}$},\\[3.50006pt]
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{\nu}\boldsymbol{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{h},&\mbox{ on $\Gamma$}\end{array}\right.

(34)

in the weak sense and setting
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{g}:=\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Gamma}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
Invoking Lemma
8.13
for the existence and uniqueness of such a solution, we thus obtain an operator
(-\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}+I)^{-1}:H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to H^{3/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

Second, applying the Korn’s inequality (see Proposition
8.8
) to the weak form of (
34
), namely

a_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{h}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3}),

where
a_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
is defined by (
25
) and

\mathcal{D}(a_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}):=H_{\#}^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3}),
where we set
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u},
we obtain the apriori estimate

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\right\rVert_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}.

Therefore,
(-\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}+I)^{-1}
can be extended to a bounded operator
from
H^{-1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
to
H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

Using, once again, an interpolation argument, we conclude that

(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}+I)^{-1}

is bounded from
L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
to
H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and is hence compact
as an operator from
L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
to itself. Thus, unity is in the resolvent set of
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

Notice that as a consequence of interpolation theorem and (
31
) and (
33
) we have

\bigl\|\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{g}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),

(35)

where the constant
C
does not depend on
\chi
.
∎

Using (
11
), we define operators
\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)},\Gamma_{1,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)},\Gamma_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
. Obviously,

\Gamma_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Gamma},\quad\Gamma_{1,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{u}=-\partial_{\nu}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{u}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{2}_{\#}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

Actually, a more general approach to defining the operators
-\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
(and thus
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
) can be developed under weaker assumptions on the regularity of the domain and the operator coefficients by considering sesquilinear forms
\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}:H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{h}):=a_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\bigl(\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g},\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{h}\bigr),\qquad\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{h}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

(36)

We have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let the boundary
\Gamma
be of class
C^{0,1}
and the coefficients of the elasticity tensor
\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff(soft)}
belong to
L^{\infty}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)})
. The forms
\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
are symmetric, non-negative, closed and densely defined in
\mathcal{E}
, respectively. Furthermore, there exists a
\chi
-independent constant
C>0
such that

|\chi|^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{g})\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

Proof.

For
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
, due to the pointwise coercivity of
\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff},
one has

\left\lVert\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})}^{2}\leq C\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{g}).

Furthermore, due to the estimate (
127
) and the trace theorem, we obtain

|\chi|^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq|\chi|^{2}\left\lVert\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})}^{2}\leq C\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{g}).

∎

Kato’s representation theorem yields the existence of (non-positive) self-adjoint
operators
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}:\mathcal{E}\supseteq\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)})\to\mathcal{E}

such that

\left\langle\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{H^{-1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{v})\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{h}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

This approach to defining Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps allows us to relax the regularity assumptions (on the boundary and the operator coefficients). However, as a result, we lose the information on the domains of these maps. In particular, we no longer have the equality

\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff})=\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm soft})=H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3}),

as in the Theorem
3.14
. We will not push this approach further, but we will use Proposition
3.16
for estimating the smallest eigenvalues of the operator
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}
. The eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps are usually referred to as Steklov eigenvalues and eigenfunctions, respectively.

3.3
Reformulation of the transmission problem in terms of a Ryzhov triple

The coupled operator
\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}
is defined by

\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\oplus\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff},\quad\mathcal{D}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}\bigr)=\mathcal{D}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\bigr)\oplus\mathcal{D}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr),

relative to the decomposition (
24
).
Equivalently it can be defined by the form

\displaystyle a_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=\int_{Y}\mathbb{A}^{\varepsilon}(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle:\overline{(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi})\boldsymbol{v}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi,\varepsilon})=\bigl\{\boldsymbol{u}\in H_{\#}^{1}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}),\,\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Gamma}=0\bigr\}.

With these definitions at hand, we can define the coupled lift operator as follows:

\Pi_{\chi}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff},\quad\Pi_{\chi}\boldsymbol{g}:=\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}\oplus\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g},\qquad\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathcal{E}.

From Theorem
3.13
we have

\ker(\Pi_{\chi})=\{0\},

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon})\cap\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{\chi})=\{0\}.

In order to describe the condition of continuity of normal derivatives from (
10
), we introduce the operator

\Lambda_{\chi,\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon^{-2}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm soft}.

Both
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
and
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm soft}
are self-adjoint on the common domain
H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and non-negative. Therefore, the operator
\Lambda_{\chi,\varepsilon}
is self-adjoint on
\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{\chi,\varepsilon}):=H^{1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
. This fact very probably belongs to the domain of folklore, still we include the proof of it as kindly shared with the authors by Dr. V. Sloushch, to whom we express our deep gratitude.

Theorem 3.17
.

Assuming there are two self-adjoint operators
{\mathcal{A}}\geq 0
and
{\mathcal{B}}\geq 0
with the same domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})
in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H},
the sum
{\mathcal{A}}+{\mathcal{B}}
is self-adjoint on the same domain.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
are positive definite. By the closed graph theorem,
{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}
and
{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}
are bounded in
\mathcal{H}
. Pick a
\kappa
such that
\|\kappa{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}\|\leq 1
. One clearly has
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\kappa{\mathcal{B}})
and
\|\kappa{\mathcal{B}}u\|\leq\|{\mathcal{A}}\boldsymbol{u}\|
for
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
.

By the Heinz inequality (see, e.g.,
[
4
, Chapter 10, Section 4.2, Theorem. 3]
),

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})^{1/2}\subset\mathcal{D}(\kappa{\mathcal{B}})^{1/2},\quad\|\kappa^{1/2}{\mathcal{B}}^{1/2}u\|\leq\|{\mathcal{A}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}\|,\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})^{1/2},

hence
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})^{1/2}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})^{1/2}
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{1/2}{\mathcal{A}}^{-1/2}
is bounded. Swapping the rôles of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})^{1/2}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})^{1/2}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{1/2}{\mathcal{B}}^{-1/2}
is bounded.

It follows that
{\mathcal{B}}^{-1/2}{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}^{-1/2}
is bounded and non-negative. Since obviously

({\mathcal{A}}+{\mathcal{B}})\boldsymbol{u}={\mathcal{B}}^{1/2}({\mathcal{B}}^{-1/2}{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}^{-1/2}+I){\mathcal{B}}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{A}})

and
{\mathcal{B}}^{-1/2}{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}}^{-1/2}+I
is boundedly invertible,
({\mathcal{A}}+{\mathcal{B}})^{-1}
is bounded and defined on the whole
\mathcal{H}
, and therefore closed. Therefore,
{\mathcal{A}}+{\mathcal{B}}
is self-adjoint on
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
, as required. ∎

Thus we can define the ”coupled” Rhyzov triple associated with the transmission boundary problem (
10
)

\left(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon},\Pi_{\chi},\Lambda_{\chi,\varepsilon}\right).

(37)

Note that, while the operators
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
and
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm soft}
are used to calculate the normal derivative on the boundary
\Gamma
, the map
\Lambda_{\chi,\varepsilon}
instead yields the jump on the boundary
\Gamma
between the normal derivative from the soft component and scaled normal derivative from the stiff component.
We can also introduce the transmission boundary operators
(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon},\Gamma_{0,\chi},\Gamma_{1,\chi,\varepsilon})
associated with (
37
).
Clearly, we have

\Gamma_{1,\chi,\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-2}\Gamma_{1,\chi}^{\rm stiff}P_{\rm stiff}+\Gamma_{1,\chi}^{\rm soft}P_{\rm soft}.

Along with these triples, we use the Definition
3.3
to define the solution operators as well as
M
-functions indexed by complex
z
in the respective resolvent sets:

\bigl(S_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(z),M_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(z)\bigr),\ \ z\in\rho\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr),\quad\bigl(S_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z),M_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)\bigr),\ \ z\in\rho\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\bigr),\quad\bigl(S_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z),M_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)\bigr),\ \ z\in\rho\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}\bigr).

Obviously, one has

\displaystyle S_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)

\displaystyle=S_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)+S_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\varepsilon^{2}z),

(38)

\displaystyle M_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)

\displaystyle=M_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)+\varepsilon^{-2}M_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\varepsilon^{2}z),\quad z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon,0}).

(39)

In the context of introduced boundary triples, the transmission boundary problem (
10
) can be formulated as finding
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f},\\[3.00003pt]
\Gamma_{1,\chi,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}=0.\end{array}\right.

The corresponding solution operator is given by the “Krein formula”

\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)=\left(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}-S_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)M_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)^{-1}S_{\chi,\varepsilon}(\overline{z})^{*},

(40)

and we know it to be the resolvent of a closed extension
\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\right)_{0,I}
of
\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}
.

Remark 3.18
.

Notice that the continuity condition
\boldsymbol{u}_{+}(y)-\boldsymbol{u}_{-}(y)=0
,
y\in\Gamma
, is built in the domain of
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
. Namely, let
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon})\dot{+}\Pi_{\chi}(\mathcal{E})
. Then

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}=\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi_{\chi}\boldsymbol{g}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}P_{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g}\right)\oplus\left(\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}\bigr)^{-1}P_{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{f}+\Pi_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}\right)

\displaystyle=:\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm soft}\oplus\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm stiff},

and

\Gamma_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm stiff})=\boldsymbol{g}=\Gamma_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm soft}).

Thus, the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})
contains pairs of functions coinciding on
\Gamma
, and the operator
\Gamma_{0,\chi}
maps such pairs to their common trace on
\Gamma
.
∎

4
Asymptotic properties of the Ryzhov triples - stiff component

The aim of this section is to provide operator asymptotics with respect to the quasimomentum
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
for the operators of the Ryzhov triple associated with the stiff component, introduced in the previous section.
As we will see, the approximation on the stiff component is the crucial part of the approximation in total (cf. Remark
7.7
below).

We also show that the eigenspace of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
corresponding, for small
\chi
, to Steklov eigenvalues of order
|\chi|^{2}
is finite-dimensional. This fact is one of the key ingredients for providing resolvent asymptotics for the transmission problem (
10
).

As we are about to see, the vector nature of the problem does not allow one to infer any asymptotic expansion for Steklov eigenvalues. However it turns out to be sufficient for our purposes to obtain the asymptotics of the resolvents of Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps, and consequently, the asymptotics of their spectral projections.

4.1
Asymptotic properties of the lift operators
