-\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}-z\left(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)^{*}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\left(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)^{*}\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}},\end{array}\right.\quad\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}\right).

(94)

Using the fact that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}
, the problem (
94
) is equivalent to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f},\\
-\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}-z\left(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}=\left(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)^{*}\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}},\end{array}\right.\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\right),\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}.\end{array}

(95)

However, by recalling (
12
) we have that
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}=\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
and thus (
95
) is equivalent to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f},\\
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}+\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+z\left(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}=-\left(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)^{*}\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}},\end{array}\right.\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\right),\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}.\end{array}

Now we define the operators
\beta_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(z):=\varepsilon^{-2}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+z(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff})^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
,
\beta_{1}=I
and introduce the transmission function defined by (
88
) to obtain

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)=\beta_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(z)+\beta_{1}\widehat{M}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z).

(96)

The operator
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)
is boundedly invertible (as can be seen by looking its imaginary part and using Corollary
8.2
) and satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
3.8
. The solution
\boldsymbol{u}
is then given by the formula (
22
), with
\boldsymbol{g}=-(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff})^{*}\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}-\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)^{-1}\bigl((\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z}))^{*}\boldsymbol{f}+\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI)^{-1}-\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)^{-1}(\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z}))^{*}&-\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{f}\\
\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}}\end{bmatrix}.

Now, one has

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}=-\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\,\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)^{-1}\bigl((\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z}))^{*}\boldsymbol{f}+\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}-\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)^{-1}(\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z}))^{*}&-\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{f}\\
\,\;\widehat{\!\!\boldsymbol{f}}\end{bmatrix}.

∎

Another insight into the operator (
92
) is obtained by considering its associated sesquilinear form, given in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.8
.

The sesquilinear form
a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
on
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
associated with the operator (
92
) is given by

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}\left(a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}\right)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{(\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}})\in\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft})\dot{+}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi},\quad\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr\},

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle:=\int_{Y_{\rm soft}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+iX_{\chi}\right)\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}}\qquad\forall\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.

Proof.

The proof goes by direct computation. ∎

Remark 5.9
.

For
\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\end{bmatrix}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}(a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, one has

\boldsymbol{u}=\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}+\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\quad\boldsymbol{v}=\mathring{\boldsymbol{v}}+\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{v}},\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}=\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{v}},

where
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft})
and
\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi}
.
With this notation at hand, the form
a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
can be written as

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y_{\rm soft}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\mathring{\boldsymbol{v}}}

\displaystyle+\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{u}}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{v}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm stiff}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{u}}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{v}}}

\displaystyle=a_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{v}})+\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{v}})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{g}_{\boldsymbol{v}})\qquad\forall\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\end{bmatrix}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}\bigl(a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}\bigr)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.

∎

The following theorem yields the final conclusion of this section, namely, the norm resolvent asymptotics in terms of the simplified operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
.

Theorem 5.10
.

There exists
C>0
, which depends only on
\sigma
and
{\rm diam}(K_{\sigma})
, such that for the resolvent of the transmission boundary problem (
10
) one has

\bigl\lVert\bigl((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI\bigr)^{-1}-\Theta_{\chi}^{*}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}-zI\right)^{-1}\Theta_{\chi}\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\qquad\forall\chi\in Y^{\prime},

where the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
is defined by (
91
), (
92
), and

\Theta_{\chi}:\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}\to\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}

is an orthogonal projection defined by

\Theta_{\chi}\left({\boldsymbol{u}}^{\rm soft}\oplus{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\rm stiff}\right)={\boldsymbol{u}}^{\rm soft}\oplus P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\rm stiff}

with respect to
L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})
inner product.

Proof.

The proof consists of using (
90
) combined with Theorem
5.7
in order to conclude that

\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}(z)=\Theta_{\chi}^{*}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}-zI\right)^{-1}\Theta_{\chi}.\qed

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

The proof is the direct consequence of Theorem
5.10
, using the fact that the (scaled) Gelfand transform is an isometry and defining

\Theta^{\rm app}:=\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}}^{-1}\Theta_{\chi}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}},\quad\mathcal{A}^{\rm app}_{{\varepsilon}}:=\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}}^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,{\varepsilon}}^{\rm app}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}}.\qed

5.4
General outlook on the approach

An alternative way to rewrite formula (
89
) is the following:

\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}(z):=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app,soft}(z)&\Bigl(\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app,soft}(z)-\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}\Bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\bigr)^{*}\\[5.0pt]
\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\Bigl(\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app,soft}(z)-\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}\Bigr)&\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\Bigl(\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app,soft}(z)-\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}\Bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\bigr)^{*}\end{bmatrix},

relative to the decomposition
\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app,soft}(z)

\displaystyle:=\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}-\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z})^{*}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}-\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)\bigl(\widehat{M}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)+\varepsilon^{-2}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+z\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z})^{*}.

The operator valued function
\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app,soft}(z)
is the solution operator (in the sense of Theorem
3.9
) to the problem of finding
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\right)
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f},\\[2.5pt]
\beta_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(z)\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}+\beta_{1}\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}=0.\end{array}\right.

where transmission operators are given by

\beta_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(z):=\varepsilon^{-2}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+z(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff})^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff},

\beta_{1}=I.

Problems like these, where the boundary conditions depend on the spectral parameter are called the boundary value problems with ”impedance”. One should note that the ”impedance” here appears in a linear manner.

On the other hand, one can observe the ”sandwich resolvent” operator
\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}(z)
defined by

\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}(z):=P_{\rm soft}\bigl((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI\bigr)^{-1}P_{\rm soft},

and use the formula (
40
) to calculate

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}(z)

\displaystyle=P_{\rm soft}\bigl((\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}-zI)^{-1}-S_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)M_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)^{-1}S_{\chi,\varepsilon}(\overline{z})^{*}\bigr)P_{\rm soft}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}-S^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)\bigl(M_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)+\varepsilon^{-2}M_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\varepsilon^{2}z)\bigr)^{-1}S_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z})^{*}.

Comparing this to the formula (
23
), we are able to conclude the following proposition.

Proposition 5.11
.

The generalised resolvent
\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}(z)
is the solution operator of the ”impedance” boundary value problem on
\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}
that consists in finding
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\right)
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{A}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}-z\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f},\\[2.5pt]
\widetilde{\beta}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(z)\Gamma_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}+\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\Gamma_{1,\chi}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}=0,\end{array}\right.

(97)

where the operators of boundary transmission are given by

\widetilde{\beta}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(z):=\varepsilon^{-2}M_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(\varepsilon^{2}z),

\widetilde{\beta}_{1}=I.

The ”impedance” of the boundary value problem (
97
) is highly nonlinear, due to the structure of the
M
-function
M_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}(z)
.
On the abstract level, both solution operators
\mathcal{R}^{\rm app,soft}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\rm soft}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
represent the so-called generalised resolvents
[
29
,
30
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
. A generalised resolvent can be equivalently characterised as either an operator of the form
P(\mathcal{A}-zI)^{-1}|_{P}
for a self-adjoint
\mathcal{A}
in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and an orthogonal projection
P
, or a solution operator of an abstract spectral boundary value problem

\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u}=z\boldsymbol{u},\quad\Gamma_{1}\boldsymbol{u}=\mathcal{B}(z)\Gamma_{0}\boldsymbol{u},

(98)

where
\mathcal{A}
is a densely defined linear operator on
P\mathcal{H}
,
(\mathcal{E},\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})
is an abstract boundary triple of
\mathcal{A}
, and
-\mathcal{B}(z)
is an analytic in the upper half-plane operator-function with positive imaginary part (i.e., an operator
R
-function) on
\mathcal{E}
, extended into the region
\Im z<0
by the identity
\mathcal{B}(z)=\mathcal{B}^{*}(\bar{z})
.

The system
(
98
)
can be thus re-cast in the form of the operator equation

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{A}_{z}\boldsymbol{u}=z\boldsymbol{u},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

where
\mathcal{A}_{z}
is a closed densely defined linear operator on
P\mathcal{H}
with domain

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{z})=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\subset P\mathcal{H}:\Gamma_{1}\boldsymbol{u}=\mathcal{B}(z)\Gamma_{0}\boldsymbol{u}\}.

The operator
\mathcal{A}_{z}
is shown to be maximal dissipative for
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
and maximal antidissipative for
z\in\mathbb{C}_{-}
.

From the point of view of generalised resolvents, one can therefore view the homogenisation procedure we have performed above as obtaining the main order term in the asymptotic expansion of the generalised resolvent
\mathcal{R}^{\rm soft}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
for every fixed
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Moreover, we point out that in order to determine the main order term of
((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI)^{-1}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, or in other words to recover the operator describing the homogenised medium, it is in fact necessary and sufficient to construct an asymptotic expansion of the generalised resolvent described above. This follows from the fact that under a natural and non-restrictive “minimality” condition the operator
\mathcal{A}
giving rise to the generalised resolvent
P(\mathcal{A}-zI)^{-1}|_{P\mathcal{H}}
is in fact uniquely determined based on the latter up to a unitary gauge transform
\Phi
such that
\Phi P\mathcal{H}=P\mathcal{H}
.

This can be viewed in the homogenisation problem at hand as taking the “down, right, up” detour in the commutative diagram

\begin{CD}((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI)^{-1}@>{\text{(1)}}>{}>(\mathcal{A}^{\text{app}}_{\chi,\varepsilon}-zI)^{-1}\\
@V{\text{(2)}}V{}V\Big\|\\
\mathcal{R}^{\rm soft}_{\chi,\varepsilon}@>{\text{(3)}}>{}>\mathcal{A}^{\text{app}}_{\chi,\varepsilon}=(\mathcal{A}^{\text{app}}_{\chi,\varepsilon})^{*}\text{ in }\mathfrak{H}\supset\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}:\\
\mathcal{R}^{\rm app,soft}_{\chi,\varepsilon}=P|_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}(\mathcal{A}^{\text{app}}_{\chi,\varepsilon}-zI)^{-1}|_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}},\end{CD}

(99)

where the double solid line represents the unitary gauge.

As far as the asymptotic analysis of the generalised resolvent
\mathcal{R}^{\rm soft}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
is concerned, the required analysis is essentially reduced to the derivation of the asymptotics of the operator
\widetilde{\beta}_{0,\chi,\varepsilon}(z)
which governs its impedance boundary conditions. This, due to (
96
), in turn reduces to a well-understood problem of perturbation theory for the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map pertaining to the stiff component of the medium and thus presents no complications.

Having said that, we point out that however appealing this argument appears, it meets two significant difficulties. Firstly, at present we don’t have an explicit way to construct the operator
\mathcal{A}^{\text{app}}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
in (
99
) for arbitrary impedance boundary conditions parameterised by a generic
-R
-operator function
\mathcal{B}(z)
in (
98
). In the problem at hand, this presents no challenge as the main order term (
93
) of the boundary operator is in fact linear in
z
. Generalised resolvents of this form have already appeared in problems of dimension reduction, most notably in the works concerned with the convergence of PDEs defined on “thin” networks to ODEs on limiting metric graphs, see, e.g.,
[
32
,
24
,
25
,
18
]
, and in particular our recent paper
[
17
]
where an approach akin to the one utilised in the present work is extended to the context of thin networks. In the area of linear elasticity in particular this analysis is thought to be applicable to the analysis of pentamodes
[
28
]
, which will be further discussed elsewhere.

Secondly and crucially, once the asymptotics of the family of generalised resolvents is obtained in some desired strong topology, the same type of convergence for the family of resolvents
((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI)^{-1}
cannot be inferred from the general operator theory. In fact, one can argue that norm-resolvent convergence of
\mathcal{R}^{\rm soft}_{\chi,\varepsilon}
only yields
strong
convergence of
((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI)^{-1}
. It is here that the specifics of the problem at hand must play a crucial rôle in the analysis, leading to a result of the type formulated in Theorem
5.5
above.

Despite the deficiencies of the general operator-theoretic outlook based on generalised resolvents explained above, we point out that this way of considering the dimension reduction problem at hand is very natural in that it presents one with a physically motivated understanding of the problem.

As the argument of
[
19
,
20
]
, see also references therein, demonstrates, generalised resolvents appear naturally in physical setups where one forcefully removes certain degrees of freedom from consideration in an otherwise conservative setting in view of simplifying the latter. Conversely, the procedure of reconstructing the self-adjoint generator of conservative dynamics must be viewed as adding those “hidden”, or concealed, degrees of freedom back in a proper way. In doing so one frequently faces a situation (and in particular, in the setup of linear elasticity discussed in the present paper) where the resulting model is drastically simplified owing to only a certain limited number of concealed degrees of freedom appearing in it in a handily transparent way. The procedure of the diagram (
99
) can be therefore seen as a non-trivial generalisation of the seminal idea of Lax and Phillips
[
26
]
, with a dissipative generator expressing the scattering properties of the system being replaced by a more general one, corresponding to an
R
-function which non-trivially depends on the spectral parameter
z
.

At the same time, as explained in
[
12
]
(see also references therein), the concept of
dilating
(in the sense of (
98
)) a generalised resolvent to a resolvent of a self-adjoint generator gives rise to the understanding of homogenisation limits in the setup of double porosity models as essentially operators on soft component of the media with singular surface potentials possessing internal structure, see also
[
14
]
where a similar argument applied to high-contrast ODEs has led to a Kronig-Penney-type model. In the problem considered in the present paper, the mentioned singular surface can be shown to be the periodic lattice of the original composite.

Moreover, the argument of
[
13
]
can be immediately invoked for the homogenised family (
92
) to obtain its functional model in an explicit form in certain explicitly constructed Hilbert space of complex-analytic functions, giving rise to a Clark-Alexandrov measure serving as the spectral measure of the family. This latter program will be pursued elsewhere, together with the study of effective scattering problems of the high-contrast composite which can be considered naturally on this basis.

6
\varepsilon
-order resolvent asymptotics of the transmission boundary problem

In this section, our aim is to further approximate the resolvent related to the transmission boundary problem and to prove Theorem
2.3
, part (a). In doing this, we will worsen the order in
\varepsilon
of the estimate, but will obtain more familiar objects in the asymptotics.

To this end, we state the following two results which are crucial for understanding the perturbation properties of the spaces
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
with respect to the quasimomentum
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
. The proof of the first result is postponed to the Appendix.

The following lemma establishes that the continuity of the orthogonal projections
P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}}
as
\chi
approaches zero.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists a constant
C>0
independent on
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
such that

\bigl\lVert P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}}-P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C|\chi|.

Our aim is to provide further approximation to the operator

\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}^{*}

which constitutes the resolvent (
90
), where the approximation error is of order not worse than
\varepsilon
.
A crucial result is the following estimate on the inverse of the transmission function:

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists
C>0
which does not depend on
\varepsilon>0
,
z\in K_{\sigma}
,
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
, such that:

\left\lVert{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}},1\right\}.

(100)

Proof.

First, note that

\displaystyle\Im\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Im z\bigl(\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)\bigr)^{*}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)+\Im z\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff},

\displaystyle\Re\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+\Re\widehat{M}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)+\Re z\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}.

For
\boldsymbol{u}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi}
we calculate using Lemma
5.1
and trace inequality:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle\Im\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=|\Im z|\left(\left\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}}+\left\langle\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq|\Im z|\bigl\lVert\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}}^{2}\geq C|\Im z|\bigl\lVert\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr\rVert^{2}_{H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff};\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{C}^{3}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\geq C|\Im z|\left\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}}^{2},

where the constant
C
depends only on
K_{\sigma}
. Thus, due to Corollary
8.2
we conclude that

\left\lVert{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C,

(101)

where the constant
C
is independent of
\chi
and
z
.

Furthermore, using Corollary
4.18
and Remark
4.19
, we infer that there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}
, which depends on
|z|
and
\sigma
such that

\left\lVert\widehat{M}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq\widetilde{C}.

By using Lemma
4.7
and the fact that
\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
is uniformily bounded we conclude that one can choose a constant
D
, independent of
\chi
,
\varepsilon
, such that for
|\chi|\geq D\varepsilon
, one has:

\left\lvert\left\langle\Re\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\right\rvert\geq C_{2}\frac{|\chi|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}}^{2}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{u}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi},

where
C_{2}
is independent of
\chi
,
\varepsilon
. For such
|\chi|
, by the application of the Corollary
8.2
we obtain

\left\lVert{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C_{2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}},

which together with (
101
) concludes the proof.
∎

Next we introduce a version of the transmission function which will appear in the final homogenisation result.

Definition 6.1
.

We refer to the operator valued function
\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z):\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\to\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
given with the following expression:

\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z):=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon^{-2}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm hom}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+z\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}+\widehat{M}_{0}^{\rm soft}(z)

(102)

as the
effective transmission function
. Associated with this we also introduce the following operator-valued function on
\mathcal{H}

\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z):=\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}P_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}-\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm eff}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}^{*},

where the
effective solution operator

\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z):\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\to\mathcal{H}_{\rm soft}
is defined by

\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z):=\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}+z\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}.

Remark 6.3
.

First we conclude that, due to the estimate
(
75
)
and the boundedness of the resolvent
(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI)^{-1},
we have

\left\lVert\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)\widehat{P}_{\chi}-\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z)\widehat{P}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C|\chi|,

where the constant
C>0
is independent of
\chi
and
z
.
Also, by using the estimate (
69
), as well as the identities (
85
), (
86
), we have

\left\lVert\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}-\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(Y_{\rm stiff};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C|\chi|.

∎

For the inverse of the effective transmission function we have an estimate similar to (
100
).

Lemma 6.4
.

There exists
C>0
which does not depend on
\varepsilon>0
,
z\in K_{\sigma}
,
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
, such that

\bigl\lVert{\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{0}\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}},1\right\}.

Proof.

The proof follows the steps of the proof of Lemma
6.2
by using Lemma
4.8
. ∎

The following lemma provides the estimate on the distance between the two transmission functions.

Lemma 6.5
.

There exists a constant
C>0
that does not depend on
\varepsilon>0
,
z\in K_{\sigma}
,
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
such that

\left\lVert\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\widehat{P}_{\chi}-\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z)\widehat{P}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C\max\left\{\frac{|\chi|^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|\chi|\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right\}

Proof.

The case of
\chi=0
is trivial. It is clear that for all
\chi\in Y^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
we have

\frac{1}{|\chi|^{2}}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{P}_{\chi}-\frac{1}{|\chi|^{2}}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}\widehat{P}_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\oint_{\gamma}z\left(\left(zI-\frac{1}{|\chi|^{2}}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\right)^{-1}-\left(zI-\frac{1}{|\chi|^{2}}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}\right)^{-1}\right)dz,

where
\gamma
is the contour provided by Lemma
4.14
.
Therefore, by applying the Theorem
4.10
(cf. Remark
4.12
), we obtain

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{P}_{\chi}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}\widehat{P}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{E}}\leq C\frac{|\chi|^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

The claim now follows from (
88
) and (
102
) by using Corollary
4.18
and Corollary
4.16
.
∎

The following lemma is crucial for obtaining
\varepsilon
-order asymptotics of the resolvent
((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI)^{-1}
and relating it to an object which incorporates the effective transmission function.

Lemma 6.6
.

There exists a constant
C>0
which does not depend on
\varepsilon>0
,
z\in K_{\sigma}
,
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
, such that

\left\lVert{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{0}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C\varepsilon.

Proof.

By a direct calculation, we see that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rm I.+II.+III.,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

where

\displaystyle\text{ I.}:=\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z)\widehat{P}_{0}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\widehat{P}_{\chi}\bigr)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{0},

\displaystyle\text{ II.}:=\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}\bigl(\widehat{P}_{\chi}-\widehat{P}_{0}\bigr),

\displaystyle\text{ III.}:=\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{P}_{0}-\widehat{P}_{\chi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigr){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{0}.

Next, we estimate using Lemma
6.2
, Lemma
6.4
and Lemma
6.5
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\text{ I.}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}},1\right\}

\displaystyle\max\left\{|\chi|,\frac{|\chi|^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}},1\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\begin{array}[]{ll}1\cdot\varepsilon\cdot 1\leq\varepsilon&\textrm{ if }|\chi|\leq\varepsilon,\\[5.0pt]
\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{|\chi|^{3}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}}=\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|}\leq\varepsilon&\textrm{ if }|\chi|\geq\varepsilon.\end{array}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right.

Furthermore, by employing Corollary
4.16
and Lemma
6.2
, one easily estimates

\left\lVert\text{ II.}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C|\chi|\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}},1\right\}\leq\left\{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\begin{array}[]{ll}|\chi|\cdot 1\leq\varepsilon&\textrm{ if }|\chi|\leq\varepsilon,\\[3.99994pt]
|\chi|\cdot\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}}=\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|}\leq\varepsilon&\textrm{ if }|\chi|\geq\varepsilon.\end{array}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right.

(103)

Similarly using again Corollary
4.16
and Lemma
6.4
we estimate:

\left\lVert\text{ III.}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}}\leq C|\chi|\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\chi|^{2}},1\right\}\leq C\varepsilon.

This concludes the proof. ∎

Finally, we are able to sum up these results into the following theorem.

Theorem 6.7
.

There exists a constant
C>0
which does not depend on
\varepsilon>0
,
z\in K_{\sigma}
,
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
, such that

\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}(z)-\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}\leq C\varepsilon,

where

\mathcal{R}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z):=\begin{bmatrix}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}-\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z){\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft,eff}(\overline{z})^{*}&-\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z){\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z)}^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\\[3.99994pt]
-\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}{\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{S}_{\chi}^{\rm soft,eff}(\overline{z})^{*}&-\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}{\widehat{Q}_{\varepsilon,\chi}^{\rm eff}(z)}^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\end{bmatrix},

(104)

and the block decomposition is relative to the decomposition
\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}
.

Proof.

The proof of this fact consists of estimating the four blocks of the matrix

\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}^{*}-\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{S}^{\rm soft,eff}_{\chi}(z)&\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\end{bmatrix}^{*}

for which all the arguments go analogously. Thus, we showcase the estimate only for one of the blocks. By using triangular inequality and Corollary
4.20
, (
85
) and Lemma
6.2
we obtain in the same way as the estimate (
103
) :

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}

\displaystyle-\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\Bigl(\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\widehat{P}_{\chi}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)}^{-1}\widehat{P}_{0}\Bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),

where
O(\varepsilon)
denotes the term of order
\varepsilon
in the operator norm
L^{2}\to L^{2}
. Using Lemma
6.6
we complete the proof.
∎

Definition 6.2
.

We define the effective operator as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}\right)

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{(\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}})\in\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft})\dot{+}\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0},\quad\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,0}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,0}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr\},

(105)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}&0\\
-\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\mathring{\Gamma}_{1,0}^{\rm soft}&-\varepsilon^{-2}\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft})\dot{+}\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}),\quad\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}:\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\bigr)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}\to\boldsymbol{f},\quad\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H},\boldsymbol{g}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\Gamma}_{1,0}^{\rm soft})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft})\dot{+}\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}(\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}),\quad\mathring{\Gamma}_{1,0}^{\rm soft}:\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}\bigr)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}+\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}\to\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}\bigr)^{*}\boldsymbol{f}+\widehat{\Lambda}_{0}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g},\quad\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H},\boldsymbol{g}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}.

Remark 6.8
.

It is straightforward to check that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff(soft)}\right)^{*}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=|Y_{\rm stiff(soft)}||\Gamma|^{-1}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}^{\rm stiff(soft)}P_{\mathcal{\widehat{H}}^{\rm stiff(soft)}_{0}}
.
∎

Recall that
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
are constant functions and
\widehat{\Lambda}_{0}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{g}=0
, for every constant function.

In a similar manner as in the Theorem
5.7
one can show the following theorem, the proof of which we omit.

Theorem 6.9
.

For every
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
, the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}
is self-adjoint and its resolvent is given, for all
z\in\rho(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}),
by the formula (
104
) relative to the decomposition
\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}.

Remark 6.10
.

The sesquilinear form
a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}
on
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
associated with the operator (
105
) is given by

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\left(a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}\right):=\left\{(\boldsymbol{u},\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}})\in\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}\oplus\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft})\dot{+}\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm soft}\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0},\quad\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,0}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,0}^{\rm soft}\boldsymbol{u}\right\},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\end{bmatrix}\right)&:=\int_{Y_{\rm soft}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{v}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,0}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,0}^{\rm stiff}\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}},\\[5.0pt]
&\hskip 150.69397pt\forall\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{v}\\
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}\bigl(a_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}\bigr).\end{aligned}

Recalling Lemma
4.9
, we can see that the same form was obtained in
[
8
]
as
\varepsilon
- approximation in the case of scalar equation by using different techniques. ∎

The following theorem provides the norm resolvent asymptotics of order
\varepsilon
with the leading order term being the ”sandwiched”-resolvent of the effective operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}
. Its proof is the direct consequence of Theorem
6.7
, cf. the proof of Theorem
5.10
.

Theorem 6.11
.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
z\in K_{\sigma}
and
\varepsilon
, such that for the resolvent of the transmission boundary problem (
10
) we have

\bigl\lVert\bigl((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI\bigr)^{-1}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Theta_{0}^{*}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}-zI\right)^{-1}\Theta_{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}\leq C\varepsilon\qquad\forall\chi\in Y^{\prime},

(106)

where the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}
is defined by (
105
), and

\Theta_{0}
is the orthogonal projection (
78
).

Proof of Theorem
2.3
(a).

The proof is the direct consequence of Theorem
6.11
, using the fact that the (scaled) Gelfand transform is an isometry and defining

\Theta^{\rm eff}:=\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}}^{-1}\Theta_{0}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}},\quad\mathcal{A}^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}:=\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}}^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,{\varepsilon}}^{\rm eff}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}}.\qed

Remark 6.12
.

In
[
10
]
Theorem
6.11
is proved by doing expansion of the smallest eigenfunction and eigenvalue of the operator
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
with respect to the quasimomentum
\chi
. As explained in the introduction of Section
4.3
, this is not possible to do when dealing with systems. Thus we did the expansion of the resolvent of the appropriately scaled operator
\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
and showed that this is enough for the proof of Theorem
6.11
. With more subtle computations we also improved the error
\varepsilon^{2/3}
, obtained in
[
10
]
to
\varepsilon
.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
we define the space
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})
as the space of functions whose scaled Gelfand transform is constant for every
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
. It is easy to see that this space consists of functions
\boldsymbol{u}
such that
\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{u})(\xi)=0
when
|\xi|_{\infty}>(2\varepsilon)^{-1}
, where
|\xi|_{\infty}=\max\{|\xi_{1}|,|\xi_{2}|,|\xi_{3}|\}
(cf. (
7
)). Here
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
denotes the Fourier variable and
\mathcal{F}(\cdot)
denotes the Fourier transform. We also introduce the space

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}:=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L_{\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}
.
Define a bilinear form
a^{\rm eff}_{\varepsilon}
by

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\left(a^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}\right):=\left\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}+\boldsymbol{u}:(\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\times\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm soft}\right\},}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} a^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}\left(\boldsymbol{u}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{v}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\right):=\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Omega^{{\varepsilon}}_{\rm soft}}\mathbb{A}_{\rm soft}\sym\nabla(\boldsymbol{u}+\hat{\boldsymbol{u}})&:\sym\nabla(\boldsymbol{v}+\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})+\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\sym\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}:\sym\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}\\[1.00006pt]
&\forall(\boldsymbol{u}+\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}),(\boldsymbol{v}+\hat{\boldsymbol{v}})\in\mathcal{D}\left(a^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}\right).\end{aligned}

It is easy to see that the scaled Gelfand transform of the form
a^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}
is equal to
a^{\rm eff}_{\chi,{\varepsilon}}
. With a different definition of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}
we can also treat the form from Remark
5.9
that defines the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
.
∎

7
The analysis on the stiff component

In this section we investigate the consequences of the estimates from the previous section.
The aim is to prove Theorem
2.3
(b). We are interested in the properties of the effective operator
(
105
) when restricted to the stiff component.
The representation formula for this operator can be obtained which brings to focus some
well-known elements related to high-contrast homogenisation (recall Lemma
4.9
, Remark
6.8
and (
74
)):

\displaystyle P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}-zI\right)^{-1}|_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}=|Y_{\rm stiff}|\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon^{-2}\left({\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-\mathcal{B}(z)\right)^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(z):=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z|Y_{\rm stiff}|I+|\Gamma|\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{M}_{0}^{\rm soft}(z),\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\mathcal{S}:\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\to\mathbb{C}^{3}\textrm{ natural inclusion}.

Notice that as a consequence of (
74
) the matrix respresentation of
\mathcal{B}
is given by (
4
).

Remark 7.1
.

Here we make a remark regarding the estimates from above and below for the operator
\mathcal{B}(z)
.
Notice that for
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
, one has:

\bigl|\left\langle\Im(\mathcal{B}(z))\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}\bigr|=\bigl|\Im(z)\bigr|\Bigl\lvert\left|Y_{\rm stiff}|\langle\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{c}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}+|\Gamma|\bigl\langle\widehat{S}_{0}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z})\boldsymbol{c},\widehat{S}_{0}^{\rm soft}(\overline{z})\boldsymbol{c}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}\Bigr\rvert\geq C|\boldsymbol{c}|,

(107)

and thus, by Corollary
8.2
one sees that

\lVert\mathcal{B}(z)^{-1}\rVert_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\leq C_{1},

where the constant
C_{1}>0
depends on the set
K_{\sigma}
. Also, one can easily see that

\bigl\lVert\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr\rVert_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\to\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\leq C_{2},

(108)

where the constant
C_{2}>0
depends only on the
\max_{z\in K_{\sigma}}|z|
.
∎

Remark 7.2
.

We will keep the same notation
\mathcal{B}(z)
for the operator of (pointwise) multiplication with the said matrix, no matter the ambient space involved.
∎

In order to pass to the real domain, what remains is to apply the inverse Gelfand transform. Before this, we introduce the following smoothing operator:
\Xi_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
defined by

{\Xi}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}:=\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{Y}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u})(y,\cdot)dy.

Next, we make a remark that the projection operator
P_{\rm stiff}
is simply a multiplication with an indicator function associated with
Y_{\rm stiff}
, namely

P_{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{u}=\mathbbm{1}_{Y_{\rm stiff}}(y)\boldsymbol{u},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{H}.

Similarly, for the operator
P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}
i.e. the orthogonal projector from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
onto
L_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}
, which is defined by (
1
), we have

P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{u}=\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{\rm stiff}^{\varepsilon}}(x)\boldsymbol{u},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

Also, for
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rm stiff}
, we have

P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\boldsymbol{u}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbbm{1}_{Y_{\rm stiff}}(y)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{|Y_{\rm stiff}|}\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\boldsymbol{u}(y)dy.

Note that

\displaystyle\Xi_{\varepsilon}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Xi_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{\rm stiff}}\boldsymbol{u}\right)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left(\int_{Y}(\mathbbm{1}_{Y_{\rm stiff}}\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u})(y,\cdot)dy\right)

(109)

\displaystyle=|Y_{\rm stiff}|\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{|Y_{\rm stiff}|}\int_{Y_{\rm stiff}}(\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u})(y,\cdot)dy\right)=|Y_{\rm stiff}|\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\bigl(\mathcal{S}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr).

We will need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.3
.

The following formula holds

\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon}}^{-1}\left(\int_{Y^{\prime}}^{\oplus}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left({\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}-\mathcal{B}(z)\right)^{-1}\mathcal{S}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\,d\chi\right)\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{|Y_{\rm stiff}|}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\Xi_{\varepsilon}P_{{\varepsilon}}^{\rm stiff},

(110)

where the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}
is defined by (
3
).

Proof.

To see this, we introduce the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\rm macro}
on
\mathcal{H}
by its associated form:

a_{\chi,\rm macro}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\int_{Y}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{v}},\quad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

By invoking the properties of the Gelfand transform (
8
), it is clear that

\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left(\int_{Y^{\prime}}^{\oplus}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\rm macro}\,d\chi\right)\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}.

By virtue of (
109
), it remains to show that

\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\mathcal{S}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}=\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\left({\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\mathcal{S}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}.

(111)

First conclusion is that for
z\in K_{\sigma}
,
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\Im\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\left({\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)=\Im\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)=-\Im\bigl(\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr),

so the operators are invertible by taking into account Corollary
8.2
and the estimates (
107
).
In order to show (
111
), we take
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathcal{H}
and consider the unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\equiv\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
to the resolvent problem

\varepsilon^{-2}\left({\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}\boldsymbol{u}-\mathcal{B}(z)\boldsymbol{u}=\mathcal{S}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\boldsymbol{f}.

By multiplying the above equation with arbitrary
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
, and integrating over
Y
one obtains

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{Y}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{{\rm i}X_{\chi}\boldsymbol{v}}-\int_{Y}\mathcal{B}(z)\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}=\int_{Y}\mathcal{S}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}.

Now, it is straightforward to check that
\boldsymbol{u}
is also the solution to the problem: find
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3})
such that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{Y}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{u}:\overline{\left(\sym\nabla+{\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)\boldsymbol{v}}-\int_{Y}\mathcal{B}(z)\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}=\int_{Y}\mathcal{S}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\#}(Y;\mathbb{C}^{3}),

which is unique. The formula (
110
) now follows from (
111
).
∎

The following lemma allows us to drop the smoothing operator
\Xi_{\varepsilon}
from the resolvent asymptotics while not making the error of higher order then
\varepsilon^{2}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
z\in K_{\sigma}
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\left(I-\Xi_{\varepsilon}\right)\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{2},

(112)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}
is a differential operator of linear elasticity with constant coefficients defined by (
3
).

Proof.

We start with the identity

\mathcal{F}({\Xi}_{{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{f})(\xi)=\mathbbm{1}_{[-\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}},\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}]^{3}}(\xi)\;\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{f})(\xi),
valid for all
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3}),

where
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
is the Fourier variable and
\mathcal{F}(\cdot)
denotes the Fourier transform (see, e.g.
[
16
, Section 2.5.3]
).
The estimate (
112
) follows from the fact that for

\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})
we have

\mathcal{F}\bigl(\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}\bigr)(\xi)=\left(\left({\rm i}X_{\xi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\xi}-\mathcal{B}(z)\right)^{-1}\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{f})(\xi).

Namely, introducing
\boldsymbol{u}(\xi):=\left(\left({\rm i}X_{\xi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\xi}-\mathcal{B}(z)\right)^{-1}\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{f})(\xi),
we have for
for
|\xi|>(2\varepsilon)^{-1}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,C_{2}^{-1/2})
:

\bigl|\boldsymbol{u}(\xi)\bigr|\leq(1-\varepsilon^{2}C_{2})^{-1}\varepsilon^{2}\bigl|\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{f})(\xi)\bigr|\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\bigl|\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{f})(\xi)\bigr|,

where
C_{2}
is given by (
108
). This concludes (
112
). ∎

Finally, we are able to prove Theorem
2.3
(b).

Proof of Theorem
2.3
(b).

The asymptotic estimate (
106
) immediately yields

\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\rm stiff}\bigl((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI\bigr)^{-1}P_{\rm stiff}-|Y_{\rm stiff}|P_{\rm stiff}\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\left({\rm i}X_{\chi}\right)^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}}\leq C\varepsilon

(113)

\displaystyle\hskip 215.2771pt\forall\chi\in Y^{\prime}.

Invoking (
110
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{Y^{\prime}}^{\oplus}

\displaystyle\left(P_{\rm stiff}\bigl((\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon})_{0,I}-zI\bigr)^{-1}P_{\rm stiff}-|Y_{\rm stiff}|P_{\rm stiff}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}({\rm i}X_{\chi})^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi}-\mathcal{B}(z)\right)^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\right)\,d\chi\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\Xi_{\varepsilon}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}.

Combining this with (
113
) and using the fact that Gelfand transform is a unitary operator, we obtain

\bigl\lVert P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}-zI\right)^{-1}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\Xi_{\varepsilon}P_{\varepsilon}^{\rm stiff}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\varepsilon,

The last step is to drop the smoothing operator for which we use Lemma
7.4
.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

The operator
\mathcal{A}_{\rm macro}-\mathcal{B}(z)
which plays the role of the leading order term in the resolvent asymptotics in Theorem
2.3
(b) is clearly a differential operator of second order with constant coefficients.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

With the arguments presented in this section we are able to recover the result for the perforated domain, obtained in
[
35
]
,
L^{2}\to L^{2}
estimate. To see this we have to take
P_{{\varepsilon}}^{\rm stiff}=I
and
\mathcal{B}(z)=z
. In order to obtain
L^{2}\to H^{1}
estimate, we would have to go one step further in the asymptotics of Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator in Section
4.3
, see also the results obtained in
[
15
,
16
]
. ∎

Remark 7.7
.

Both operators
\mathcal{A}^{\rm app}_{{\varepsilon}}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}
are pseudodifferential. However,
\mathcal{A}^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}
is simpler (on the stiff component). If one wants to obtain approximation of the resolvent of the original problem, we cannot further simplify (homogenise) the operator
\mathcal{A}^{\rm eff}_{{\varepsilon}}
. Indeed, on the stiff component the approximation is already
fully homogenised
(cf. Remark
7.6
), while on the soft component it cannot be further simplified. On the other hand for the operator
\mathcal{A}^{\rm app}_{{\varepsilon}}
it seems that by going further at the expansion in Section
4.3
one would be able to obtain simpler approximating operator (simplification would again be done on the stiff component), while keeping the precision
{\varepsilon}^{2}
. It can be shown that the obtained operator would again be pseudodifferential (even on the stiff component). We refrained ourselves from doing this, for the efficiency of the exposition. We emphasize the fact that when dealing with high contrast the limit or approximating operator is always
\varepsilon
-dependent. In the case of the limit operator this is seen through the fact that it is defined on the spaces which have additional (micro) variable (see
[
42
]
), representing the oscillations on the soft component. Thus we can say that, in the case of high contrast, the homogenisation is necessarily
partial
. From the point of view of wave propagation this is expected since on the soft inclusions the wave length (in the regime where we are interested) is proportional to the size of the cell, while on the stiff matrix the wave length is much bigger than the size of the cell. ∎

7.1
The dispersion relation

The assertion of Theorem 6.11, as well as a more precise (at the cost of being much more involved) statement of Theorem 5.7, pertains to the asymptotic behaviour of the resolvent in the whole space. In applications however, and in particular in applications to periodic problems, it is often desirable to relate the spectrum of the problem to the so-called wave vector, or equivalently quasimomentum
\chi,
of the problem at hand. Indeed, the dispersion relation, which expresses the mentioned relationship, becomes of a paramount importance when the question of which monochromatic waves are supported by the medium at hand, as well as when the group velocity of wave packets spreading in the medium is brought to the forefront of investigation. The latter question is to arise naturally in the context of metamaterials, to which the model considered in the present paper is thought to be intimately related (the precise mathematical formulation of this relationship will however be discussed elsewhere; see also
[
12
]
and references therein for a related discussion), as in these both the phase and group velocity need to negate,
[
40
]
.

Definition 7.1
.

We refer to the operator valued function defined by the following formula:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z):=-\bigr(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)

\displaystyle\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}+zI

(114)

\displaystyle=-\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+\widehat{M}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}(z)\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}

as the
dispersion function
associated with the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
. Similarly, we define the
effective dispersion function
associated with the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z):=-\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\widehat{Q}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}(z)

\displaystyle\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}+zI

(115)

\displaystyle=-\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}+\widehat{M}_{0}^{\rm soft}(z)\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}.

Remark 7.8
.

Notice that

\bigl(\mathcal{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)-zI\bigr)^{-1}=-\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widehat{Q}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*},\qquad\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z)-zI\bigr)^{-1}=-\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigl(\widehat{Q}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z)\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{0}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}.

By comparing this with resolvent formulas: (
89
) and (
104
), one can see that these operators are, in fact, resolvents of the appropriate operators sandwiched with projections onto the stiff component:

P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}-zI\bigr)^{-1}|_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}}=\bigl(\mathcal{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)-zI\bigr)^{-1},\qquad P_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}-zI\bigr)^{-1}|_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}=\bigl(\mathcal{K}^{\rm eff}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)-zI\bigr)^{-1}.

∎

Denote by
\left\{\psi_{1}^{\chi},\psi_{2}^{\chi},\psi_{3}^{\chi}\right\}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi}
an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of
\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
, and with
\{\nu_{1}^{\chi},\nu_{2}^{\chi},\nu_{3}^{\chi}\}
the associated set of eigenvalues.
Next, we note that the set
\{\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{1}^{\chi},\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{2}^{\chi},\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{3}^{\chi}\}
forms a basis for
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
, so the function
\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
is represented in this basis with the vector of scalars
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
as follows:

\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\alpha_{i}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{i}^{\chi}.

(116)

Furthermore, we denote with
\{\Psi_{1}^{\chi},\Psi_{2}^{\chi},\Psi_{3}^{\chi}\}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}
the contravariant basis of
\{\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{1}^{\chi},\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{2}^{\chi},\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{3}^{\chi}\}
, namely the set of functions for which we have

\bigl\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{i}^{\chi},\Psi_{j}^{\chi}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm stiff}}=\delta_{ij},\quad i,j=1,2,3,

where
\delta_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker delta. One can easily check that

\Psi_{j}^{\chi}=\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\psi_{j}^{\chi},\quad j=1,2,3.

Thus we find the following expression for the coefficients
\alpha_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
in (
116
):

\alpha_{j}=\langle\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,\chi}\widehat{\boldsymbol{u}},\psi_{j}^{\chi}\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi}},\quad j=1,2,3.

Furthermore, we calculate the matrix
\mathbb{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)
of the operator
\mathcal{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)
in the basis
\{\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{i}^{\chi},i=1,2,3\}:

\bigl[\mathbb{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)\bigr]_{ij}=\Bigl\langle\mathcal{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\psi_{j}^{\chi},\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\psi_{i}^{\chi}\Bigr\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}}.

By invoking the formulas (
15
), (
14
) and (
114
), the dispersion function can be expressed as

\mathcal{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z):=-\bigl(\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{*}\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\varepsilon^{-2}\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}+\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}+z\left(\hat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\right)^{*}\hat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}+z^{2}\left(\hat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\right)^{*}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}\hat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}.

Denote now by
(\eta_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
(\varphi_{k}^{\chi})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}
the eigenvalues and associated eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}
. We write the expansion of the resolvent of
\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}
with respect to its spectral projections:

\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{0,\chi}^{\rm soft}-zI\bigr)^{-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\eta_{k}-z}\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\chi},\quad\mathcal{P}_{k}^{\chi}\boldsymbol{f}:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{f},\varphi_{k}^{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}\varphi_{k}^{\chi}.

After straightforward computation we obtain:

\displaystyle\bigl[\mathbb{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)\bigr]_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{-2}\nu^{j}_{\chi}\bigl\langle\psi_{j}^{\chi},\mathbb{H}_{\chi}\psi_{i}^{\chi}\bigr\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm stiff}_{\chi}}-\bigl\langle\widehat{\Lambda}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\psi_{j}^{\chi},\mathbb{H}_{\chi}\psi_{i}^{\chi}\bigr\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm stiff}_{\chi}}

(117)

\displaystyle-z\bigl\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\psi_{j}^{\chi},\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\mathbb{H}_{\chi}\psi_{i}^{\chi}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}}{\eta_{k}-z}\bigl\langle\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\psi_{j}^{\chi},\varphi_{k}^{\chi}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}\bigl\langle\varphi_{k}^{\chi},\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm soft}\mathbb{H}_{\chi}\psi_{i}^{\chi}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{\rm soft}}.

where we have denoted by
\mathbb{H}_{\chi}
the self-adjoint operator
\bigl((\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff})^{*}\widehat{\Pi}_{\chi}^{\rm stiff}\bigr)^{-1}:\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi}\to\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{\chi}
.

Similarly, by using (
115
), one can find the matrix representation
\mathbb{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm{eff}}(z)
of the effective dispersion function
\mathcal{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z)
in the canonical basis
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
to be the following:

[\mathbb{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm{eff}}(z)]_{ij}:=\left\langle\mathcal{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z)e_{j},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}=-\varepsilon^{-2}\bigl\langle\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}e_{j},e_{i}\bigr\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}-\bigl\langle\widehat{M}_{0}^{\rm soft}(z)e_{j},e_{i}\bigr\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}.

We recall the formula (
74
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\left\langle\widehat{M}_{0}^{\rm soft}(z)e_{j},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}

\displaystyle=z\left\langle\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{j},\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm soft}}+z^{2}\left\langle\left(\mathcal{A}_{0,0}^{\rm soft}-zI\right)^{-1}\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{j},\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm soft}}

\displaystyle=z\left\langle\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{j},\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm soft}}+z^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left\langle\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{j},\varphi_{k}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm soft}}\left\langle\varphi_{k},\Pi_{0}^{\rm soft}e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm soft}}}{\eta_{k}-z}

\displaystyle=z|Y_{\rm soft}|\delta_{ij}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}}{\eta_{k}-z}[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{j}[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{i}.

where
(\eta_{k},\varphi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are the eigenpairs of the operator
\mathcal{A}_{0,0}^{\rm soft}
.
Finally, we have

\left\langle\left(\mathcal{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}(z)-zI\right)e_{j},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left\langle\Lambda_{\chi}^{\rm hom}e_{j},e_{i}\right\rangle_{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\rm stiff}}-z\delta_{ij}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}}{\eta_{k}-z}[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{j}[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{i}.

Definition 7.2
.

The dispersion relation on the stiff component for the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm app}
is the equation

\det\left(\mathbb{K}^{\rm app}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)-z|Y_{\rm stiff}|\mathbb{I}\right)=0,

relating the parameters
z
and
\chi
, where the matrix
\mathbb{K}_{\chi,\varepsilon}(z)\in\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3}
is given with (
117
). The
effective
dispersion relation is given by

\det\bigl(\mathbb{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}-\mathcal{B}(z)\bigr)=0,

where (cf. Lemma
4.9
)

\mathbb{A}_{\chi,\varepsilon}^{\rm eff}:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}({\rm i}X_{\chi})^{*}\mathbb{A}_{\rm macro}{\rm i}X_{\chi},\qquad\mathcal{B}(z)=zI+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}}{\eta_{k}-z}[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{j}[\overline{\varphi_{k}}]_{i}.

8
Appendix

8.1
Operator theory

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a closed, densely defined linear operator on a complex Hilbert space, such that

\lvert\left\langle\mathcal{A}x,x\right\rangle\rvert\geq C\lVert x\rVert^{2},\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
and
\ker(\mathcal{A}^{*})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}).

Then the inverse
\mathcal{A}^{-1}
exists and

\lVert\mathcal{A}^{-1}\rVert\leq C^{-1}.

Proof.

First, we show that
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})}=\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
. In order to do this, we take
x\in\ker(\mathcal{A}^{*})
. Since
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}),
one has

0=\bigl\lvert\left\langle x,\mathcal{A}^{*}x\right\rangle\bigr\rvert=\bigl\lvert\left\langle\mathcal{A}x,x\right\rangle\bigr\rvert\geq C\lVert x\rVert^{2}

and therefore
x=0
. Therefore,

\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})}=\bigl(\ker(\mathcal{A}^{*})\bigr)^{\perp}=\{0\}^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{A}
is an injection. Thus
\mathcal{A}^{-1}
exists. For
y\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
we put
x=\mathcal{A}^{-1}y
. Thus, one has

\lVert x\rVert^{2}=\lVert\mathcal{A}^{-1}y\rVert^{2}\leq C^{-1}\bigl\lvert\left\langle y,\mathcal{A}^{-1}y\right\rangle\bigr\rvert\leq C^{-1}\lVert y\rVert\lVert\mathcal{A}^{-1}y\rVert.

From this we have the claim. ∎

Corollary 8.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a closed, densely defined linear operator on a complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{*})
. Denote by
\Re\mathcal{A}
and
\Im\mathcal{A}
the real and imaginary part of the operator
\mathcal{A},
respectively. Assume that
\max\bigl\{\bigl\lvert\langle\Im\mathcal{A}x,x\rangle\bigr\rvert,\bigl\lvert\langle\Re\mathcal{A}x,x\rangle\bigr\rvert\bigr\}\geq C\lVert x\rVert^{2}
for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
for some
C>0.

Then the inverse
\mathcal{A}^{-1}
exists and

\lVert\mathcal{A}^{-1}\rVert\leq C^{-1}.

Proof.

The claim follows immediately from the bound

\bigl\lvert\langle\mathcal{A}x,x\rangle\bigr\rvert=\sqrt{\bigl\lvert\langle\Re\mathcal{A}x,x\rangle\bigr\rvert^{2}+\bigl\lvert\langle\Im\mathcal{A}x,x\rangle\bigr\rvert^{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\geq\max\bigl\{\bigl\lvert\langle\Im\mathcal{A}x,x\rangle\bigr\rvert,\bigl\lvert\langle\Re\mathcal{A}x,x\rangle\bigr\rvert\bigr\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}).\qed

8.2
Auxiliary estimates

Here we state some of the results which we use in the main text. In this part we state various version of Korn’s and trace inequalities.

The following proposition is the special situation of the classical trace theorem.

Proposition 8.3
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
\boldsymbol{g}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})
there is an extension
\boldsymbol{G}\in H_{\chi}^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})
satisfying

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{G}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(Y_{\rm stiff(soft)};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\right\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}^{3})}.

Next is a well known Korn’s inequality (see, e.g.,
[
31
]
).

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded open set with Lipschitz boundary. There exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})
, we have

\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\left(\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left\lVert\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right),

(118)

where the constant
C
depends only on the domain
\Omega
.

The following version of Korn’s inequality is also well known. We will provide its proof by a contradiction from Proposition
8.4
.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded open set with Lipschitz boundary and
B\subset\partial\Omega
,
|B|>0
.
Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})
we have

\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\left(\lVert\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(B;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right),

(119)

where the constant
C
depends only on
\Omega
and
\Gamma
.

Proof.

By the inequality (
118
) we see that it suffices to show that

\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C\left(\lVert\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(B;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right).

Suppose the opposite, namely that we have a sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)^{3}
,
\lVert\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}=1
such that

\lVert\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}>k\left(\lVert\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lVert\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\rVert_{L^{2}(B;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right),\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

By (
118
), the sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and therefore converges weakly in
H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})
and strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})
to some
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})
,
\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}=1.
By lower semicontinuity of the norm, we have

\lVert\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\lVert\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{k}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}=0.

(120)

Also, since
\boldsymbol{u}_{k}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{u}
in
H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3}),
by trace compactness we infer that
\boldsymbol{u}|_{\Gamma}=0.
By (
120
) one has
\boldsymbol{u}(x)=\boldsymbol{A}x+\boldsymbol{c}
for some
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3}_{\rm skew}
,
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
(see, e.g.,
[
31
]
). Since
|B|>0
this implies that
\boldsymbol{A}=0
,
\boldsymbol{c}=0
.
∎

The following version of Korn’s inequality is also well known and is a direct consequence of
[
31
, Theorem 2.5]
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded open set with Lipschitz boundary. There exists
C>0
dependent only on
\Omega
such that for every
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})
the estimate

\lVert\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{w}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C||\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}||_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3\times 3})}

holds with
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{A}x+\boldsymbol{c}
, where

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&d&a\\
-d&0&b\\
-a&-b&0\\
\end{bmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{c}=\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\\
c_{2}\\
c_{3}\end{bmatrix},\quad c_{j}=\int_{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}),\quad j=1,2,3,

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{u}_{3}),\quad b=\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}-\partial_{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{3}),\quad d=\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}),

The following proposition is simple to prove.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded open set. There exist constants
C_{0},C_{1}>0
, independent of
\chi\in Y^{\prime}
, such that

C_{0}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq\left\lVert{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\chi y}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\leq C_{1}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{u}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3})}\qquad\forall\chi\in Y^{\prime},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{3}).

(121)

Proof.

We clearly have (due to
|{\rm e}^{i\chi y}|=1
)
